Figure 6b shows the evolution of main sensitivity index of proxy output T_arrive to every [SOURCE: Page_148]

parameter. Except in the downstream part of the field, CV(T_arrive) is always less than 20%: [SOURCE: Page_148]

this proxy output is not sensitive to any of the parameters in the major part of the field, except [SOURCE: Page_148]

downstream where it is mainly sensitive to Ks (SKs = 48%). In the upstream part of the field, [SOURCE: Page_148]

despite the high contribution of parameter H$_0$ and k to the variance of this proxy output with [SOURCE: Page_148]

STk and STH$_0$ values higher than 40%, CV(T_arrive) is less than 15%. For the upstream [SOURCE: Page_148]

section used in Table 2, CV(T_arrive) is about 7 % : the value of T_arrive is more or less [SOURCE: Page_148]

constant in the first upstream sections of the field and its value does not depend on the studied [SOURCE: Page_148]

parameters, but may be to other parameters such as the field slope or the injected water flow. [SOURCE: Page_148]

In the perspective of the estimation of models parameters through inverse modelling, the [SOURCE: Page_148]

145 [SOURCE: Page_148]

monitoring of T_arrive in upstream part of the field may not provide enough information to [SOURCE: Page_149]

estimate H$_0$ and k parameters despite their high sensitivity indices. On the contrary, the high [SOURCE: Page_149]

value of CV(T_arrive) in the downstream section used in Table 2 may provide enough [SOURCE: Page_149]

information for estimating the most sensitive parameters (Ks, then H$_0$ and k). [SOURCE: Page_149]

As for T_arrive, only T_submersion output in downstream section has a CV higher than 20% [SOURCE: Page_149]

with a high value of SD=2.47 hours (see Table 2). Total sensitivity indices of Ks and  are [SOURCE: Page_149]

greater than 10% (see Fig 6c). As a consequence, we may suggest that the monitoring of [SOURCE: Page_149]

T_submersion in a downstream cross section of the field could be useful for the estimation of [SOURCE: Page_149]

these two parameters. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Figure 6d shows that the CV’s values for the H_integ output are close to 20% upstream and [SOURCE: Page_149]

reach 83% downstream. Two parameters have main effects close to 50% upstream: these are [SOURCE: Page_149]

parameters k and H$_0$. Downstream, parameter Ks with SKs close to 80% is the most sensitive [SOURCE: Page_149]

parameter,the second one being parameter  with S =15% Therefore data of H_integ [SOURCE: Page_149]

monitored in the two sections of plot can improve the estimation of all these parameters. [SOURCE: Page_149]

From the overall results presented above and looking ahead for the estimation of models [SOURCE: Page_149]

parameters with inverse modelling approach through the in situ monitoring of variables [SOURCE: Page_149]

related to the dynamic of the water height at the field surface during irrigation, we can suggest [SOURCE: Page_149]

an experimental monitoring design sensitive to the parameters to estimate. The monitoring of [SOURCE: Page_149]

hydrographs during irrigation in two cross sections of the field, one located upstream and the [SOURCE: Page_149]

other one located downstream enables the computation of simple proxy outputs whose [SOURCE: Page_149]

variations are related to the value of the parmaters to estimate, as shown in this section and [SOURCE: Page_149]

summarized in Table 3. [SOURCE: Page_149]

### 4. Conclusion

Surface irrigation models provide a good understanding of irrigation system behavior through [SOURCE: Page_150]

predicting the main hydraulic processes such as water propagation over the soil surface and [SOURCE: Page_150]

water infiltration into the soil. These processes are governed by modules requiring different [SOURCE: Page_150]

parameters. The knowledge of these parameters depends on their nature. Some of them are [SOURCE: Page_150]

easy to measure directly as those related to the geometry of the field or those related to the [SOURCE: Page_150]

irrigation management such as irrigation duration, injected discharge etc... On the other hand, [SOURCE: Page_150]

some parameters are difficult to measure at the field scale, due to their spatial variability (as [SOURCE: Page_150]

for the soil hydraulic conductivity) or their conceptual nature (as for the roughness coefficient [SOURCE: Page_150]

of Manning for surface water flow). In that case, parameters values must be defined, either [SOURCE: Page_150]

from automatic fitting methods that may involves inverse modeling approach, or by using [SOURCE: Page_150]

predefined values found in the literature. The accuracy needed on the knowledge of [SOURCE: Page_150]

parameters values will depend on the sensitivity of the model to these parameters. Sensitivity [SOURCE: Page_150]

analysis methods are usefull tools in order to assess this sensitivity and/or to define [SOURCE: Page_150]

experimental monitoring designed for an accurate estimation of unknown parameters. [SOURCE: Page_150]

In this study we investigated the global sensitivity analysis (GSA) of a surface irrigation [SOURCE: Page_150]

model called “Calhy” using the EFAST method. Six parameters (Manning roughness 147 [SOURCE: Page_150]

coefficient k, amount of water stored at the soil surface H$_0$, soil water depletion , soil [SOURCE: Page_151]

hydraulic conductivity at saturation Ks, soil water suction at the infiltration front Pini, soil [SOURCE: Page_151]

depth Z) related to the modeling of water flow at the soil surface and to the modeling of water [SOURCE: Page_151]

infiltration into the soil must be known. The GSA was performed in two step depending on [SOURCE: Page_151]

the goal of the GSA and on the king of studied variables. The first set of studied variables are [SOURCE: Page_151]

operational outputs of the model involved in the water balance computation and useful for the [SOURCE: Page_151]

analysis of the modeled irrigation system. These variables are the mean water height during [SOURCE: Page_151]

the irrigation at a given location in the field, the amount of infiltrated water at the end of the [SOURCE: Page_151]

irrigation at a given location in the field and the mean infiltrated water amount at the end of [SOURCE: Page_151]

the irrigation at the field scale. The sensitivity of these variables to the model parameters has [SOURCE: Page_151]

been assessed through the calculation of sensitivity indices and through the calculation of the [SOURCE: Page_151]

coefficient of variation of these variables. Results show that four parameters have a [SOURCE: Page_151]

significant effect on the mean water height at the soil surface during irrigation: Ks, k, H$_0$ and [SOURCE: Page_151]

. Only two parameters have an impact on the variance of the infiltrated water: K s and . [SOURCE: Page_151]

Parameters Z (soil depth) and Pini (soil suction at the infiltration front) are the less sensitive [SOURCE: Page_151]

parameters. [SOURCE: Page_151]

The second set of variables were called “proxy variables” and were calculated from the [SOURCE: Page_151]

evolution of the water height at the soil surface during the irrigation in two cross sections of [SOURCE: Page_151]

the field, one located upstream and the other one locate downstream. The goal of this second [SOURCE: Page_151]

GSA step was to identify an experimental monitoring system to be setup in the field and [SOURCE: Page_151]

designed for an accurate estimation of unknown parameters. We showed a high sensitivity of [SOURCE: Page_151]

the proxy variables to parameters k, H$_0$, Ks and  and we showed that this sensitivity [SOURCE: Page_151]

depends on the variable and on its location in the field. These results suggest that an [SOURCE: Page_151]

experimental monitoring system based on the monitoring of the water height at the soil [SOURCE: Page_151]

surface in an upstream section of the field and in a downstream section of the field could be [SOURCE: Page_151]

appropriate for the estimation of 4 parameters over 6. Results show also that the two [SOURCE: Page_151]

148 [SOURCE: Page_151]

remaining unknown parameters, Z and Pini have little influence on the output of interest [SOURCE: Page_152]

studied in the first step of the GSA. [SOURCE: Page_152]

152 [SOURCE: Page_155]

Inversion du modèle Calhy : “Estimation Of Soil Hydrodynamic Parameters And Soil Hydraulic Roughness Through Inverse Modelling And Flooding Irrigation Experiments” [SOURCE: Page_156]

Le chapitre précédent a permis d’une part d’identifier les paramètres auxquels le modèle CALHY est sensible, et d’autre part de définir un dispositif de mesure relativement simple à mettre en œuvre et permettant de mesurer des variables caractérisant les irrigations et présentant une forte sensibilité aux paramètres. En se basant sur les résultats de l’analyse de sensibilité présentée dans le précédent chapitre, nous avons déployé un dispositif expérimental adapté sur différentes parcelles agricoles afin d’estimer par méthode inverse les quatre principaux paramètres hydrodynamiques et hydrauliques du sol présents dans le modèle Calhy : [SOURCE: Page_156]

    la conductivité hydraulique à saturation Ks (m s-1),     le coefficient de frottement hydraulique k (m^1/3 s-1),     le paramètre  (m^3 m-3) qui représente la différence de teneur en eau entre l’état initial avant le début de l’irrigation et l’état saturé,     le paramètre H$_0$ (m) qui représente l’eau retenue par les microreliefs de la surface du sol. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Méthodes et démarches [SOURCE: Page_156]

Deux parcelles de caractéristiques différentes ont été étudiées. Pour la 1ere parcelle située sur le domaine du Merle, 5 irrigations successives ont été étudiées. Pour la seconde parcelle située chez un agriculteur, deux irrigations différentes ont été suivies. L’évolution de la hauteur de la lame d’eau présente à la surface du sol lors de l’irrigation est mesurée sur deux sections transversales à l’écoulement : une section se situe dans la partie amont de la parcelle, une section se situe dans la partie aval. À partir des données expérimentales sur l’évolution de la hauteur d’eau H(t) ruisselée à la surface de la parcelle, les variables secondaires appelées proxy variables qui ont été définies dans le précédent chapitre ont été calculées. Ces variables sont celles que le modèle CALHY devra reconstituer au mieux en ajustant de façon automatique la valeur des différents paramètres. L’algorithme d’ajustement utilisé est la méthode SIMPLEX. L’estimation des paramètres a été conduit sur l’ensemble des irrigations sauf une irrigation sur le 1er site qui a été utilisée pour la validation. [SOURCE: Page_156]

La performance de l’approche proposée a été évaluée en trois étapes. Dans un premier temps la cohérence des valeurs estimées des paramètres a été étudiée : 153 [SOURCE: Page_156]


   Le paramètre Ks estimé au cours de plusieurs irrigations et sur le même site doit être stable,    le paramètre k estimé doit évoluer de façon cohérente avec l’état de végétation : il doit diminuer lorsque la prairie se développe. [SOURCE: Page_157]

Dans un second temps, la méthode a été évaluée en comparant les résultats de la simulation avec des données mesurées mais non utilisées dans la procédure d’inversion. Ces données sont les teneurs en eau du sol, les hydrogrammes complets de ruissellement en amont et en aval de la parcelle, ainsi que l’avancée du front de ruissellement à la surface de la parcelle lors de l’irrigation. Des critères statistiques d’erreur (critère RMSE : Root Mean Square Error et critère de Nash-Sutcliffe) ont été calculés sur les hydrogrammes de ruissellement. [SOURCE: Page_157]

Finalement la validation au sens strict est réalisée en comparant les simulations à des données expérimentales qui ne sont pas issues des irrigations utilisées dans la procédure d’ajustement. Ces données sont une des irrigations suivie sur le 1er site expérimental, ainsi que des données issues des travaux de (Mailhol and Merot, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_157]

Résultats [SOURCE: Page_157]

Les résultats montrent que : [SOURCE: Page_157]

    le paramètre estimé Ks est constant sur une parcelle donnée, quelle que soit l’irrigation [SOURCE: Page_157]

utilisée pour l’ajustement. Il est de plus comparable à des valeurs trouvées [SOURCE: Page_157]

précédemment par (Bader et al, 2010), [SOURCE: Page_157]

    le paramètre estimé k varie de façon cohérente avec le stade de développement de la [SOURCE: Page_157]

prairie. Les ordres de grandeurs sont similaires aux valeurs que l’on peut trouver dans [SOURCE: Page_157]

la littérature. Les valeurs trouvées sont cependant plus faibles que les valeurs trouvées [SOURCE: Page_157]

précédemment par (Bader et al, 2010), [SOURCE: Page_157]

    les paramètres Ks et k sont estimés avec une faible incertitude, alors que le paramètre [SOURCE: Page_157]

 a été estimé avec une incertitude modérée, [SOURCE: Page_157]

    le paramètre H$_0$ est estimé avec une forte incertitude, cependant ce paramètre n’a pas [SOURCE: Page_157]

d’influence sur le bilan hydrique ou la dynamique globale de l’irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_157]

154 [SOURCE: Page_157]

Lors de l’évaluation, on montre que les critères d’erreurs sont faibles : les critères de Nash- [SOURCE: Page_158]

Sutcliffe sont toujours supérieurs à 0.85 et peuvent atteindre la valeur de 1.00 pour la [SOURCE: Page_158]

reconstitution des hydrogrammes de drainage. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Lors de la validation, le modèle permet également de bien reconstituer les données mesurées, [SOURCE: Page_158]

qu’il s’agisse des hydrogrammes de ruissellement (critère de Nash-Sutcliffe supérieur à 0.90, [SOURCE: Page_158]

RMSE inférieure à 9 mm) ou de la propagation du front de ruissellement à la surface du sol [SOURCE: Page_158]

(RMSE compris entre 5.1 m et 7.4 m). [SOURCE: Page_158]

Nous concluons que l’algorithme d’inversion (méthode du SIMPLEX) associé à un dispositif [SOURCE: Page_158]

expérimental relativement léger basé sur le suivi en continu de la lame d’eau dans les parties [SOURCE: Page_158]

amont et aval de la parcelle, complété par la connaissance du débit d’irrigation injecté, permet [SOURCE: Page_158]

d’estimer les paramètres hydrodynamiques et hydrauliques qui ont le plus d’influence sur la [SOURCE: Page_158]

dynamique de l’irrigation et son bilan hydrique. Cette approche peut être déployée sur de [SOURCE: Page_158]

nombreuses parcelles ou calans si celles-ci fonctionnent selon les hypothèses du modèle, à [SOURCE: Page_158]

savoir des parcelles de forme régulière (rectangulaire) où la lame d’eau se propage dans une [SOURCE: Page_158]

seule direction et où l’eau peut s’évacuer librement en aval de la parcelle (existence d’un [SOURCE: Page_158]

canal de colature) : elle permettrait d’une part de faire des bilans précis pour chaque parcelle [SOURCE: Page_158]

et d’autre part de spatialiser les propriétés du sol. Cette méthode nécessite cependant de bien [SOURCE: Page_158]

connaitre le débit injecté. Le chapitre suivant qui présente les résultats des enquêtes effectuées [SOURCE: Page_158]

chez de nombreux agriculteurs, montre que le débit effectivement injecté peut différer [SOURCE: Page_158]

significativement des estimations des agriculteurs ou des débits théoriques associés aux ASA [SOURCE: Page_158]

concernées. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Ce chapitre a été soumis sous forme d’article à la revue Irrigation Science en juin 2016. [SOURCE: Page_158]

155 [SOURCE: Page_158]

Estimation of soil hydrodynamic parameters and soil hydraulic roughness through inverse modelling and flooding irrigation experiments. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Mohamed ALKASSEM ALOSMAN$_1$, Stéphane RUY$_1$*, S. BUIS$_1$, P. LECHARPENTIER$_1$, J.C. BADER$_2$, F. CHARRON$_3$, Albert OLIOSO$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_159]

## ABSTRACT :

Surface irrigation; the most widespread irrigation system in the world; is highly water- [SOURCE: Page_159]

consuming and therefore needs to be optimized to save water. Surface irrigation models can [SOURCE: Page_159]

be used for this purpose but they require soil parameters at the field scale, which is a difficult [SOURCE: Page_159]

task. We propose a method to estimate effective soil parameters at the field scale by [SOURCE: Page_159]

combining a runoff-infiltration model, namely the CALHY model (Bader et al., 2010), [SOURCE: Page_159]

monitoring of surface irrigation and an automatic fitting algorithm based on the SIMPLEX [SOURCE: Page_159]

method. The kinematic wave approximation and the Manning Strickler equation were used for [SOURCE: Page_159]

runoff simulation, and the Green and Ampt model to simulate soil water infiltration. Four [SOURCE: Page_159]

parameters were estimated: the saturated hydraulic conductivity Ks, the hydraulic roughness [SOURCE: Page_159]

coefficient k, the soil water depletion , and the soil surface depressional storage H           0. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Evolution of the water height at two cross sections of the irrigated field was measured with [SOURCE: Page_159]

automatic probes. Ks and k were estimated with a low uncertainty, whereas the estimation [SOURCE: Page_159]

uncertainty of parameter H$_0$ was of the order of magnitude of the parameter value. All [SOURCE: Page_159]

experimental data were very well simulated. Performance criteria (Nash coefficient and [SOURCE: Page_159]

RMSE) were calculated from the discrepancy between measured and simulated data, and their [SOURCE: Page_159]

values calculated during the fitting stage were found to be similar to those calculated during [SOURCE: Page_159]

the validation stage. The method was validated using several datasets. It can be used to [SOURCE: Page_159]

estimate the effective soil parameters governing flooding irrigation from simple [SOURCE: Page_159]

measurements in the field. It can be applied on a large number of fields to analyze current [SOURCE: Page_159]

flooding irrigation practices and to propose optimization strategies. [SOURCE: Page_159]

156 [SOURCE: Page_159]

KEYWORDS : flooding irrigation, hay field, grasslands, runoff, infiltration, inverse [SOURCE: Page_160]

modeling, kinematic wave, Green and Ampt model. [SOURCE: Page_160]

## 1- INTRODUCTION

Irrigated agriculture represents about 20% of the total surface of cultivated crop lands (FAO, [SOURCE: Page_161]

2011), but accounts for about 70% of global freshwater withdrawals and for 90% of [SOURCE: Page_161]

consumptive water uses (Siebert et al., 2010). The surface of irrigated crop lands is expected [SOURCE: Page_161]

to expand in the future in order to contribute to the 70% increase in global crop production [SOURCE: Page_161]

required to cope with the rise in world population by 2050 (Bruinsma, 2009). This will induce [SOURCE: Page_161]

an increase in water demand for irrigation whereas fresh water resources are very unevenly [SOURCE: Page_161]

distributed with an increasing number of countries or regions suffering from water scarcity [SOURCE: Page_161]

(Bruinsma, 2009). A mitigating factor could be the optimization of current irrigated systems [SOURCE: Page_161]

in order to save water and then to expand the irrigated surfaces on a constant basis of water [SOURCE: Page_161]

withdrawals. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Surface irrigation is the oldest form of irrigation system throughout the world. It is also the [SOURCE: Page_161]

most widespread, accounting for about 85% of the total irrigated crop lands in 2007 according [SOURCE: Page_161]

to (FAO, 2016). Large volumes of water are injected at the plot surface, which may lead to [SOURCE: Page_161]

large amounts of water loss either by drainage below the crop rooting depth or by runoff from [SOURCE: Page_161]

the plot. It is therefore a highly water-consuming system which can result in a low water use [SOURCE: Page_161]

efficiency. Optimization in surface irrigation systems could be reached through improved [SOURCE: Page_161]

management practices including changes in field geometry, injection discharge or cut-off [SOURCE: Page_161]

times (Clemmens, 2009; Moravejalahkami et al., 2009). In order to accomplish this [SOURCE: Page_161]

optimization, a model of surface irrigation is needed. Such models should account for the two [SOURCE: Page_161]

main processes driving the irrigation of an agricultural field, namely water infiltration into the [SOURCE: Page_161]

soil and runoff propagation at the field surface, and the parameters of these models must [SOURCE: Page_161]

therefore be measured or estimated. These parameters include infiltration parameters and field [SOURCE: Page_161]

surface roughness parameters, which are difficult to obtain due to spatial variability in the [SOURCE: Page_161]

field and to the interaction between these two processes which act in opposite ways: runoff [SOURCE: Page_161]

158 [SOURCE: Page_161]

over the field surface tends to decrease the soil water recharge, whereas infiltration into the [SOURCE: Page_162]

soil tends to increase this recharge. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Infiltration is the most important function that has to be estimated or measured in order to [SOURCE: Page_162]

determine the volume of water needed and the irrigation duration. Parameters for describing [SOURCE: Page_162]

soil water infiltration are related to the hydraulic conductivity curve and the water retention [SOURCE: Page_162]

curve but depend on the soil water infiltration model. The difficulty in estimating the [SOURCE: Page_162]

infiltration parameters on a physical basis is a major obstacle to improving performance [SOURCE: Page_162]

(Ebrahimian et al., 2010; Elliott et al., 1983; Gillies and Smith, 2005; Weibo et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_162]

Moreover, the required irrigation duration is also influenced by the flow resistance (soil [SOURCE: Page_162]

topography, vegetation cover), since for a given water discharge during irrigation, the [SOURCE: Page_162]

hydraulic roughness factor is related to the water height and to the runoff velocity (Bader et [SOURCE: Page_162]

al., 2010; Clemmens, 2009; Galbiati and Savi, 1995; Gillies and Smith, 2015). Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_162]

irrigation duration and water volume needed for surface irrigation should be based on an [SOURCE: Page_162]

accurate estimation of both infiltration and soil surface parameters. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Current methods for measuring infiltration such as single- and double-ring infiltrometers are [SOURCE: Page_162]

unable to give an accurate estimation of the soil water infiltration parameters at the field scale [SOURCE: Page_162]

because of soil spatial variability, which is usually large. Moreover they are costly and time- [SOURCE: Page_162]

consuming (Lee and Walker, 1993; Weibo et al., 2012). Inverse methods are therefore usually [SOURCE: Page_162]

preferred in order to estimate “average” infiltration parameters at the field scale. In these [SOURCE: Page_162]

methods, a runoff-infiltration mathematical model is used to simulate observed data such as [SOURCE: Page_162]

the propagation of the runoff front and of the recession front at the soil surface, the runoff [SOURCE: Page_162]

hydrographs, the evolution of the water height at the soil surface at specific places in the field, [SOURCE: Page_162]

etc. Infiltration parameters of the mathematical model are automatically calculated to obtain [SOURCE: Page_162]

the best fit between observed and simulated data (Clemmens, 2009; Ebrahimian et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_162]

Gillies et al., 2006; Tiscareno-Lopez et al., 1993; Walker, 2005). According to these authors, [SOURCE: Page_162]

the more data we have, the better the fit, but a good estimation of the parameters is not 159 [SOURCE: Page_162]

ensured when only a limited set of data is available. It is thus very important to use or develop [SOURCE: Page_163]

a method which employs minimal accessible (available) data. As it is costly and time [SOURCE: Page_163]

consuming to obtain a lot of experimental data, several authors tried to identify the minimal [SOURCE: Page_163]

data set required to obtain a “good” estimation of the parameters. [SOURCE: Page_163]

(Elliott and Walker, 1982) developed the two-point method for furrow, basin and border [SOURCE: Page_163]

irrigation. They assumed that measuring the advance curve of the runoff front in two points at [SOURCE: Page_163]

the plot surface, in the mid and last points of the layout (plot or furrow), was sufficient to [SOURCE: Page_163]

estimate the infiltration parameters. [SOURCE: Page_163]

The method is based on a balance volume method (eq. 1) for the mass conservation, a power [SOURCE: Page_163]

law function for the advanced phase (eq. 2) and the modified Kostiakov equation (Smith, [SOURCE: Page_163]

1972) for the infiltration rate (eq. 3).The mass conservation is written in its integral form as : [SOURCE: Page_163]

=           +                                        (39) where Q$_0$ is the inflow discharge (m^3/min); t is the time from the start of inflow (min); x is the [SOURCE: Page_163]

water front advance (m); s is the distance reached by the advance front at time t (m); A 0 is the [SOURCE: Page_163]

cross-section area of inflow (m²); σy is an empirical surface profile shape factor; and f is the [SOURCE: Page_163]

cumulative infiltration per unit length (m^3 m-1). [SOURCE: Page_163]

The empirical relationship between the time and the position of the runoff front is needed. [SOURCE: Page_163]

(Elliott and Walker, 1982) used a power law function: [SOURCE: Page_163]

=                                              (40) [SOURCE: Page_163]

where p (m min-r) and r (-) are constants. [SOURCE: Page_163]

The modified Kostiakov equation for the cumulative infiltration is: [SOURCE: Page_163]

( )=        +                                        (41) [SOURCE: Page_163]

in which a (-), k (m² min-a), and f^0 (m² min-1) are constant coefficients. [SOURCE: Page_163]

When the parameters of eq. (1) and eq. (2) are known, the parameters a and k of eq.(3) can be [SOURCE: Page_163]

calculated when the position x^1 and x^2 of the runoff front is measured at two times t^1 and t^2. [SOURCE: Page_163]

However in this method the variations in the advance time between these two points which 160 [SOURCE: Page_163]

are caused by variation in discharge or hydraulic resistance are not accounted for, which may [SOURCE: Page_164]

induce an inaccurate estimation of infiltration parameters (Clemmens et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_164]

The one point method was proposed by (Shepard et al., 1993) for furrow irrigation. In this [SOURCE: Page_164]

method, the Philip infiltration equation (Philip, 1969) is used to express the infiltration rate, [SOURCE: Page_164]

which reduces the number of infiltration parameters that has to be estimated. The runoff [SOURCE: Page_164]

dynamic is measured at only one point in the field, which is usually located at the end of the [SOURCE: Page_164]

furrow. [SOURCE: Page_164]

An alternative one-point method was presented by (Mailapalli et al., 2008) for furrow [SOURCE: Page_164]

irrigation. As in the two-point method (Elliott and Walker, 1982), the mass conservation [SOURCE: Page_164]

equation, the water advance curve at the soil surface and the infiltration rate are described [SOURCE: Page_164]

using the volume balance method, a power law function and the modified Kostiakov equation [SOURCE: Page_164]

respectively. In order to reduce the required data (one measurement point instead of two [SOURCE: Page_164]

points), the volume balance equation was simplified by proposing a function including the [SOURCE: Page_164]

two exponents’ parameters; the exponents of the power advance relationship, see eq. (2) and [SOURCE: Page_164]

that of the modified Kostiakov equation, see eq. (3). The estimation of the two parameters a [SOURCE: Page_164]

and r could be affected by minimizing this function to zero. [SOURCE: Page_164]

(Ebrahimian et al., 2010) proposed two alternative methods. The first method was derived [SOURCE: Page_164]

from that of (Shepard et al., 1993): they calculated the exponent of the power law function [SOURCE: Page_164]

describing the advance of the runoff front from two points in the advance curve instead of [SOURCE: Page_164]

assuming that it was constant. The second method is similar to that of (Elliott and Walker, [SOURCE: Page_164]

1982), but they assumed that the exponent of the advance curve function was constant at 0.5 [SOURCE: Page_164]

and they measured the position of the advance curve at a single point. [SOURCE: Page_164]

A multilevel calibration technique proposed by (Walker, 2005) used an inflow and outflow [SOURCE: Page_164]

hydrograph in order to estimate the infiltration parameters and the value of the flow roughness [SOURCE: Page_164]

coefficient of Manning n. Compared to the previous methods, this new procedure provided a [SOURCE: Page_164]

better fit of the advance runoff curve and of the recession runoff curve. [SOURCE: Page_164]

161 [SOURCE: Page_164]

All the above-mentioned methods require the calculation or estimation of a surface shape [SOURCE: Page_165]

factor σy in order to compute the surface volume over the soil surface according to the volume [SOURCE: Page_165]

balance equation. This parameter is assumed to vary from 0.7 to 0.8 (Weibo et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_165]

Some methods proposed to calculate σy with the following equation: [SOURCE: Page_165]

1 =                                     (4) 1+ In eq. (4), the value of the  parameter is either assumed to be constant, equal to 0.25 [SOURCE: Page_165]

according to (Gillies and Smith, 2005) or equal to 0.45 according to (Bautista et al., 2009), or [SOURCE: Page_165]

is fitted as a function of system properties and evolves with time and with the distance of [SOURCE: Page_165]

water advance (E. Bautista et al., 2012; Valiantzas, 1993). All these approaches assume that [SOURCE: Page_165]

this factor is constant with time. This may lead to errors in the estimation procedures [SOURCE: Page_165]

(Clemmens, 2009; Gillies et al., 2006). Moreover ignoring the variation in irrigation discharge [SOURCE: Page_165]

Q$_0$ during irrigation could compromise the accuracy of results when the volume balance [SOURCE: Page_165]

method is used (Bautista et al., 2009c; Gillies et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_165]

(Wang et al., 2007) tried to avoid the estimation of the surface storage shape factor by [SOURCE: Page_165]

proposing a method to estimate the soil infiltration parameters of border irrigation, based on [SOURCE: Page_165]

the volume balance and collected data of spatial and temporal variation of the water depth at [SOURCE: Page_165]

the soil surface at several locations along the field. [SOURCE: Page_165]

(Bader et al., 2010) proposed a new method to estimate the infiltration parameters and soil [SOURCE: Page_165]

surface parameters for submersion irrigation of hay fields. The Green and Ampt equation [SOURCE: Page_165]

(Green and Ampt, 1911) was used for the infiltration rate, and the kinematic wave [SOURCE: Page_165]

approximation or the diffusive wave approximation of the St Venant equation for the runoff [SOURCE: Page_165]

dynamic at the soil surface. The experimental data set was composed of the observed water [SOURCE: Page_165]

advance curve all along the field, the runoff discharge at the inlet and outlet of the field. Fitted [SOURCE: Page_165]

parameters were the saturated hydraulic conductivity, the soil water storage capacity, the [SOURCE: Page_165]

Manning Strickler roughness coefficient k and the soil surface detention (water height [SOURCE: Page_165]

retained by the soil surface micro-topography). In the remainder of this article, the Manning 162 [SOURCE: Page_165]

Strickler roughness coefficient, usually denoted k, will be used as the flow resistance [SOURCE: Page_166]

coefficient. This coefficient is the inverse of the Manning roughness coefficient n = 1/k. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Several combinations of infiltration and flow resistance parameters can fit the same water [SOURCE: Page_166]

advance curve for a given discharge (Clemmens, 2009; Clemmens et al., 1998): the [SOURCE: Page_166]

propagation of the runoff front may slow down because of either a high infiltration rate or a [SOURCE: Page_166]

low flow roughness (a high value of the Manning Strickler roughness coefficient, k). This is a [SOURCE: Page_166]

common problem with inverse methods, known as equifinality. Thus having enough [SOURCE: Page_166]

information about the roughness factor k could make the infiltration parameter the only one [SOURCE: Page_166]

that controls the advance time. When dealing with furrow irrigation, the water front [SOURCE: Page_166]

propagates at the soil surface on a more or less bare surface, and values of the roughness [SOURCE: Page_166]

coefficient can be assumed or found in different tables. These approaches were used in the [SOURCE: Page_166]

above-mentioned methods. When dealing with flood irrigation, the water front propagates at [SOURCE: Page_166]

the soil surface through the vegetation, and it is difficult to estimate the roughness coefficient: [SOURCE: Page_166]

for irrigated pasture, the Manning Strickler roughness coefficient becomes increasingly [SOURCE: Page_166]

significant and tends to vary with the plant height, the water depth and the flow velocity [SOURCE: Page_166]

(Gillies and Smith, 2015; Robertson et al., 2004; Wilson, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_166]

In (Bader et al., 2010; Wang et al., 2007), the authors included more data such as water depth [SOURCE: Page_166]

in several locations, runoff discharge and water volume losses from the plot to avoid the [SOURCE: Page_166]

equifinality problem. These additional data are however very difficult to obtain in border [SOURCE: Page_166]

irrigation (Weibo et al., 2012) or cannot be precisely determined. Moreover, any errors in [SOURCE: Page_166]

measurements used in the estimation procedures could lead to a wrong estimation of the [SOURCE: Page_166]

infiltration and roughness parameters (Gillies et al., 2006; Robertson et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_166]

The aim of the present study is to propose a new method to estimate infiltration and soil [SOURCE: Page_166]

surface parameters during flooding irrigation of hay fields. The method is based on a [SOURCE: Page_166]

mathematical model of the runoff dynamic at the field surface in which the infiltration rate is [SOURCE: Page_166]

163 [SOURCE: Page_166]

described by the Green and Ampt equation and the water flow at the surface is described by [SOURCE: Page_167]

the kinematic approximation of the St Venant equation. The parameters to be estimated are [SOURCE: Page_167]

the two parameters of the infiltration equation, i.e. the field saturated hydraulic conductivity [SOURCE: Page_167]

Ks and the soil water storage ∆θ, and the two parameters of the runoff propagation equation, [SOURCE: Page_167]

i.e. the Manning-Strickler roughness coefficient k and the soil surface detention H$_0$. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Estimation of the parameters is based on an inverse modeling method and on observed data [SOURCE: Page_167]

that can be easily measured at two points located at the surface of the field. [SOURCE: Page_167]

164 [SOURCE: Page_167]

2- Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_168]

2.1.Model of border irrigation ; Runoff, Advance and Infiltration, [SOURCE: Page_168]

In this study we used the CALHY model which is a physically based model originally [SOURCE: Page_168]

developed in an MS Excel spreadsheet by (Bader et al., 2010). The model was recoded in the [SOURCE: Page_168]

Matlab framework under the form of function code accompanied with a call-script including [SOURCE: Page_168]

all the required input data. The model describes (i) the water propagation (runoff) at the field [SOURCE: Page_168]

surface and (ii) the soil infiltration during an irrigation event. Moreover it can provide useful [SOURCE: Page_168]

information about the irrigation dynamics. The surface runoff is one dimensional and is [SOURCE: Page_168]

described by the kinematic wave approximation of the Saint Venant equation (5). The relation [SOURCE: Page_168]

between the water height and the flow velocity is explained by the Manning-Strickler [SOURCE: Page_168]

relationship (6). The soil infiltration is modeled by the Green and Ampt equation (7). The [SOURCE: Page_168]

governing set of equations is as follows: [SOURCE: Page_168]

+          =−                               (5) [SOURCE: Page_168]

=    ×         (0;   −     ) / ( )/                   (6) [SOURCE: Page_168]

(     −   )(        + ) =      1+                                              (7) [SOURCE: Page_168]

( , )=           ( , )                            (8) [SOURCE: Page_168]

where Ks is the soil saturated hydraulic conductivity (m s-1), k is the Manning-Strickler [SOURCE: Page_168]

Roughness factor (m^1/3 s-1), ∆θ is the soil water depletion (m^3 m-3) represented by the [SOURCE: Page_168]

difference between the saturated water content θs and the initial water content θi, Pini is the [SOURCE: Page_168]

matric suction behind the infiltration front (m) and H$_0$ is the water height retained at the soil [SOURCE: Page_168]

surface by the vegetation and the soil micro-topography (m). H$_0$ is the depression storage, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_168]

the water volume that has to be filled at the soil surface before runoff starts (Amoah et al., [SOURCE: Page_168]

2013). The other inputs required include the field slope I (m m-1), the inflow rate per unit field [SOURCE: Page_168]

width Qi (m^3 m-1 s-1) and the field length (m). [SOURCE: Page_168]

165 [SOURCE: Page_168]

The main outputs are: f the instantaneous soil infiltration flow (m s -1), F the cumulative [SOURCE: Page_169]

infiltration (m), H the water height at the soil surface (m) and Qrl the water discharge per unit [SOURCE: Page_169]

width of the field (m^3 m-1 s-1). [SOURCE: Page_169]

The CALHY model is solved according to the numerical scheme described by (Bader et al., [SOURCE: Page_169]

2010), which is a time explicit scheme with a constant time step ∆t. All variables are [SOURCE: Page_169]

calculated at each time step along a regular grid with a ∆x space step along the runoff [SOURCE: Page_169]

propagation direction. [SOURCE: Page_169]

2.2.Site of measurements [SOURCE: Page_169]

Field location [SOURCE: Page_169]

The study was carried out in the Crau region in southern France where flooding or border [SOURCE: Page_169]

irrigation is used in order to irrigate more than 12000 ha of grassland. In this region hay fields [SOURCE: Page_169]

are levelled lengthwise, with a gentle slope ranging from 2.5‰ to 21‰ but usually less than [SOURCE: Page_169]

4‰. The irrigation discharge is injected all along the upstream width of the plot until the [SOURCE: Page_169]

runoff front reaches the end of the plot. Then the irrigation discharge is stopped and the [SOURCE: Page_169]

amount of water at the field surface is evacuated as infiltration or runoff from the plot. In [SOURCE: Page_169]

order to save water, farmers usually stop the irrigation when the runoff front reaches a certain [SOURCE: Page_169]

percentage of the total field length, This decision is based on their expert knowledge and takes [SOURCE: Page_169]

into account the discharge value, the field slope and the runoff velocity (Bader et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_169]

Merot et al., 2008b). [SOURCE: Page_169]

A typical flooding irrigation (conducted in the field or simulated by the model) results in the [SOURCE: Page_169]

propagation of a runoff front at the soil surface from the upstream part to the downstream part [SOURCE: Page_169]

of the field, and then in the disappearance of water after the end of irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_169]

Soil description [SOURCE: Page_169]

In this study, 7 irrigation events were monitored in two different sites. The first site is the “1J [SOURCE: Page_169]

plot” located in the experimental facilities of the “Domaine du Merle” located near the town [SOURCE: Page_169]

of Salon de Provence and the second site is situated near the Entressen mere. The soil of the 166 [SOURCE: Page_169]

first site is a calcic ferresiallitic luvisol, stony and stratified (Bouteyre and Duclos, 1994; [SOURCE: Page_170]

Merot et al., 2008b; Mohammed et al., 2016), composed of (i) an upper loam horizon [SOURCE: Page_170]

accumulated from the sediments which are brought by irrigation water from the Durance [SOURCE: Page_170]

river, poor in gravels (about 5%) with a thickness of 10-15 cm, (ii) an intermediate layer of [SOURCE: Page_170]

loamy sand (from 15 to 75% stones) and (iii) a fissured hardened stony sandy layer which is [SOURCE: Page_170]

found at a depth between 0.45 and 0.65 m. [SOURCE: Page_170]

In the second site, the soil is complex between loam-clay with presence of a clay substrate and [SOURCE: Page_170]

a hydromorphous loamy upper horizon, rich in organic matter. The soil depth is limited to 0.2 [SOURCE: Page_170]

m due to the presence of the same hardened horizon (Bouteyre and Duclos, 1994). [SOURCE: Page_170]

2.3.Monitoring of the water height at the field surface [SOURCE: Page_170]

A mobile device was used to monitor the water height at the field surface during the [SOURCE: Page_170]

irrigation. During each irrigation event, the evolution of the water height at the soil surface [SOURCE: Page_170]

was measured with automatic probes and data loggers in two cross sections of each plot. [SOURCE: Page_170]

These sections were located at 10% (upstream section) and 90% (downstream section) of the [SOURCE: Page_170]

total field length. Water height measurements were replicated three times at each section with [SOURCE: Page_170]

Campbell Scientific CS616 soil moisture probes which were installed vertically with a 10 cm [SOURCE: Page_170]

insertion into the soil. These probes were calibrated and a linear relationship between the [SOURCE: Page_170]

recorded signal of the probes and the water height was obtained, with a mean R² of 0.99 as [SOURCE: Page_170]

shown in Fig. (1). As the length of the CS616 moisture probes is 30 cm, the maximum water [SOURCE: Page_170]

height that could be measured was about 20 cm, the usual maximum water height obtained [SOURCE: Page_170]

during flood irrigation of hay crops in the Crau area being about 10 cm. The probes were [SOURCE: Page_170]

connected to a Campbell Scientific CR1000 data logger and measurements were made and [SOURCE: Page_170]

stored every 30s. [SOURCE: Page_170]

167 [SOURCE: Page_170]

Figure (1): Typical calibration curve of the soil moisture probes CS616 in order to convert the probe signal (in µs) into water height (in mm) at the soil surface. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Figure (2): Time evolution of water levels during an irrigation event in two locations in the field. The first blue curve was obtained in the upstream section of the field, and the second red curve in the downstream section of the field. Proxy-variables are given on this figure. [SOURCE: Page_171]

The typical evolution with time of the water height in the upstream and downstream sections [SOURCE: Page_171]

is presented in Fig. (2). In both sections, the curves are qualitatively similar. To begin with, [SOURCE: Page_171]

the water height is constant and equal to zero from the beginning of the irrigation to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

arrival of the runoff front at the measurement point. For this irrigation, the arrival time of the [SOURCE: Page_171]

irrigation front at the upstream section is about 0.5 hour and about 4.7 h in the downstream [SOURCE: Page_171]

section. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Then we observe an increase in the water height up to a constant value that corresponds to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

maximum value of the water height obtained during the steady state flow regime. This [SOURCE: Page_171]

increase is sharper in the upstream section than in the downstream section, due to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

168 [SOURCE: Page_171]

dispersion of the runoff front along the field slope. The maximum value of the water height in [SOURCE: Page_172]

the upstream section is greater than the one in the downstream section. In the upstream [SOURCE: Page_172]

section the water discharge is almost the same as the injected irrigation discharge as this [SOURCE: Page_172]

section is located near the entrance of the flow in the field, whereas in the downstream section [SOURCE: Page_172]

this discharge is equal to the injected irrigation discharge minus the cumulative infiltration [SOURCE: Page_172]

flow from the upstream to the downstream section. At the end of the steady state flow regime, [SOURCE: Page_172]

we observe a sharp (respectively smooth) decrease in the water height in the upstream section [SOURCE: Page_172]

(respectively downstream section) from its maximum value to zero. This is due to the arrival [SOURCE: Page_172]

time of the recession front which develops at the end of the irrigation. As for the runoff front, [SOURCE: Page_172]

the dispersion along the field slope is responsible for the difference in the shape of the [SOURCE: Page_172]

recession curves between the two sections. All these characteristics can be described by a [SOURCE: Page_172]

kinematic wave model such as the CALHY model. [SOURCE: Page_172]

2.4.Measurement of soil characteristics [SOURCE: Page_172]

Soil samplings with augers were carried out before and after each event in order to determine [SOURCE: Page_172]

the Soil samplings with augers were carried out before and after each event in order to [SOURCE: Page_172]

determine the soil water content profile and the soil water depletion ∆θ. For each event, three [SOURCE: Page_172]

replicates at the upstream section and three replicates at the downstream section were used to [SOURCE: Page_172]

calculate the mean soil water content. The soil volumetric water content was corrected by [SOURCE: Page_172]

taking into account the amount of stones in the soil profile. [SOURCE: Page_172]

The suction at the infiltration front, which is parameter Pini in the CALHY model, was not [SOURCE: Page_172]

measured. As several authors (Bader et al., 2010; Galbiati and Savi, 1995; Kidwell et al., [SOURCE: Page_172]

1997; Risse et al., 1994) showed that the matric suction Pini has only a slight influence on the [SOURCE: Page_172]

simulation of the rates and the amounts of infiltration and runoff compared to other [SOURCE: Page_172]

parameters such as those caused by Ks, k and ∆θ, this parameter was computed as a function [SOURCE: Page_172]

of the soil water content depletion with the relation obtained from data presented by (Bader et [SOURCE: Page_172]

al., 2010). 169 [SOURCE: Page_172]

2.5.Measurement of soil characteristics [SOURCE: Page_173]

Injected water discharge Qi was measured at the canal that brought the irrigation water into [SOURCE: Page_173]

the inlet of the field at the beginning of irrigation with a portable current-meter. The advanced [SOURCE: Page_173]

runoff was determined for two irrigation events: the observed position of the runoff front was [SOURCE: Page_173]

marked every 30 minutes by an operator walking along the field at 5 locations in the cross [SOURCE: Page_173]

section of the flow direction with plastic sticks. At the end of the experiment, the position of [SOURCE: Page_173]

each plastic stick was measured with a decameter and a GPS device. The precision of the [SOURCE: Page_173]

measurements is evaluated at ±2 m. Photographic monitoring was also conducted to analyze [SOURCE: Page_173]

the vegetation development (data not shown). [SOURCE: Page_173]

The field data values of the CALHY parameters measured during each irrigation event in two [SOURCE: Page_173]

locations are presented in Table (1). [SOURCE: Page_173]

2.6.Experimental data used for the fitting algorithm (“proxy-data”) [SOURCE: Page_173]

Several studies in soil and hydrology science showed that the confidence in a numerical [SOURCE: Page_173]

model requiring parameter fitting increases when a large amount of experimental data are [SOURCE: Page_173]

170 [SOURCE: Page_173]

used during the fitting process (Gillies and Smith, 2005; Moravejalahkami et al., 2009; [SOURCE: Page_174]

Sedaghatdoost and Ebrahimian, 2015; Walker, 2005). However, it is very important to use [SOURCE: Page_174]

independent data to avoid estimation errors resulting from their interdependence (Buis et al., [SOURCE: Page_174]

2011). Because of the autocorrelation of the measurements of the evolution of the water [SOURCE: Page_174]

height in the upstream or downstream section in the field, we decided not to use the whole [SOURCE: Page_174]

dataset in the inverse modeling approach. We extracted some key variables from this data set [SOURCE: Page_174]

(see Fig (1)) that we called “proxy-variables”. These variables are: [SOURCE: Page_174]

   T_arrive (h) is the arrival time of the runoff front, [SOURCE: Page_174]

   T_submersion (h) is the duration of the submersion, [SOURCE: Page_174]

   H_max (mm) is the maximal value of the water height, [SOURCE: Page_174]

   H_integral (mm h) is defined as :             =                       ( ) [SOURCE: Page_174]

   These variables were calculated for the two measurement sections. [SOURCE: Page_174]

2.7.The inversion modelling approach [SOURCE: Page_174]

Based on the SIMPLEX algorithm (Nelder and Mead, 1965), an inverse modeling approach [SOURCE: Page_174]

was used to estimate parameters Ks, k, H$_0$ and  of the CALHY model. The data used for the [SOURCE: Page_174]

fit are the proxy-variables defined above. The objective function to be minimized was based [SOURCE: Page_174]

on the weighted least squares method (Charnes et al., 1976), computed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_174]

,    −   , (   ) _           =                                           (42) , [SOURCE: Page_174]

where j is the variable number, k is the variable position, Obs and Sim are the observed and [SOURCE: Page_174]

simulated variable values, θ is the vector of the parameter values and σj^2 is the variance of [SOURCE: Page_174]

Obsj. The values of σj^2 for each proxy variable are given in Table (2) and identified from the [SOURCE: Page_174]

experimental variance calculated from the three signals recorded at each position in situ for [SOURCE: Page_174]

each variable of interest. [SOURCE: Page_174]

H-max 0.125       0.121               0.125          81              0.0125           0.25            0.25            36 σj^2 (h^2)        (h^2)                (mm^2)     (mm^2.h^2)              (h^2)            (h^2)            (mm^2)         (mm^2.h^2) [SOURCE: Page_175]

Table (2). The variance σj^2 used in the calculation of the objective function for each proxy variable at the two measurement sections in the field. [SOURCE: Page_175]

The optimization scheme is based on evaluating the objective function (OF) iteratively [SOURCE: Page_175]

through successive values of the set of parameters . For the first iteration an initial set of [SOURCE: Page_175]

parameters’ values is sampled randomly inside a specified value range presented in Table (3). [SOURCE: Page_175]

When the algorithm finds a new minimum OF, the parameter values are used for the [SOURCE: Page_175]

following iterations. [SOURCE: Page_175]

The inversion algorithm stops for a given repetition when convergence is achieved or when [SOURCE: Page_175]

the maximum number of iterations has been reached. Convergence is achieved when the [SOURCE: Page_175]

difference between two successive estimations of OF is less than a predefined tolerance [SOURCE: Page_175]

criterion. [SOURCE: Page_175]

This optimization scheme is repeated 20 times; each one starts with a new set of initial [SOURCE: Page_175]

parameter values within their interval limit. The final value of the parameters is the one [SOURCE: Page_175]

corresponding to the minimum value of the objective function between all the repetitions. [SOURCE: Page_175]

The validation of the proposed approach was performed in three successive steps summarized [SOURCE: Page_176]

in Table (4): coherence and physical meaning of the fitted parameters, verification using the [SOURCE: Page_176]

experiments used for the inverse modeling approach, validation performed using experiments [SOURCE: Page_176]

not used for the inverse modeling approach and data from the literature. [SOURCE: Page_176]

Coherence and physical meaning of the fitted parameters: [SOURCE: Page_176]

   For a given field, the hydraulic conductivity at saturation, Ks, is constant over time. We [SOURCE: Page_176]

therefore analyzed the variability of the value of Ks from one estimation to the other on [SOURCE: Page_176]

each experimental site. [SOURCE: Page_176]

   The Manning-Strickler roughness coefficient k should be qualitatively related to the hay [SOURCE: Page_176]

development and/or biomass: the more biomass there is, the more friction there is and the [SOURCE: Page_176]

lower k is. Moreover the value of k should be less than the value for an agricultural bare [SOURCE: Page_176]

soil; it should be maximum for an irrigation following the harvest of the field, and it [SOURCE: Page_176]

should be minimum for the last irrigation just before the harvest. All the experiments used [SOURCE: Page_176]

for the inverse modeling approach were used during this step. [SOURCE: Page_176]

Evaluation of the simulated hydrograph and runoff front on the situations used for [SOURCE: Page_176]

calibration: [SOURCE: Page_176]

   We compared the estimated and the measured soil water depletion  ; [SOURCE: Page_176]

   We compared the simulated and the measured hydrograph H(t) in two cross sections of [SOURCE: Page_176]

the field. Measured hydrographs are raw data that were not used directly during the [SOURCE: Page_176]

inverse modeling approach. The reconstitution of the measured hydrographs was [SOURCE: Page_176]

evaluated using the root mean square error RMSE (see eq. (10)) and the Nash-Sutcliffe [SOURCE: Page_176]

criteria (see eq. (11)) which were computed as follows : [SOURCE: Page_176]

∑      (   −       ) =                                                 (10) [SOURCE: Page_176]

     We compared the simulated and the measured advance of the runoff front during [SOURCE: Page_177]

irrigation. The experimental data of the advance of the runoff front were not used during [SOURCE: Page_177]

the inverse modeling approach, but they were obtained during the same irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_177]

All the experiments used for the inverse modeling approach were used during the comparison [SOURCE: Page_177]

of  and H(t). The advance of the runoff front was measured during experimentation A$_5$ [SOURCE: Page_177]

only. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Evaluation of the simulated hydrograph and runoff front on situations not used for [SOURCE: Page_177]

     We simulated irrigation A$_4$ that was not used during the inverse modeling approach. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Parameters Ks, k, H$_0$ and  used for this direct simulation came from the parameters [SOURCE: Page_177]

obtained from irrigation A$_5$. The comparison between simulated and measured data was [SOURCE: Page_177]

performed upon the advance of the runoff front and the hydrographs H(t) at two sections [SOURCE: Page_177]

of the field. [SOURCE: Page_177]

     Lastly, we simulated the experiments of (Mailhol and Merot, 2008) obtained on the first [SOURCE: Page_177]

experimental site. [SOURCE: Page_177]

For all inversion procedures and all irrigation events, convergence of the fitting algorithm was [SOURCE: Page_180]

achieved for more than 15 times over 20 repetitions. For the remaining repetitions, the [SOURCE: Page_180]

algorithm stopped because the maximum number of iterations had been reached. [SOURCE: Page_180]

The OF values calculated for the first iteration ranged between 300 and 550, and decreased to [SOURCE: Page_180]

0.44 for irrigation A$_5$ and 0.05 for irrigation A$_1$ after convergence, see Table (6). Parameters Ks [SOURCE: Page_180]

and k were always estimated with a low uncertainty, whereas the two remaining parameters [SOURCE: Page_180]

∆θ and H$_0$ were estimated with a higher uncertainty. [SOURCE: Page_180]

3.2.Coherence and physical meaning of the fitted parameters [SOURCE: Page_180]

For a given experimental site, the fitted value of the hydraulic conductivity at saturation K s [SOURCE: Page_180]

did not change from one irrigation to another. For the first site, values of Ks ranged between [SOURCE: Page_180]

1.34 10-6 and 1.5 10-6 m s-1. This parameter value varied within a very narrow range. An [SOURCE: Page_180]

average value of 1.4 10-6 ms-1 was calculated for this site, which is in accordance with the [SOURCE: Page_180]

values obtained by Bader et al. (2010) and (Mailhol and Merot, 2008) on the same plot. For [SOURCE: Page_180]

the second site, the two estimations of Ks were also close to each other, with an average value [SOURCE: Page_180]

of 1.1 10-7 m s-1, about one order of magnitude less than for the first experimental site, [SOURCE: Page_180]

indicating that the soil of the second experimental site may be less permeable than the soil of [SOURCE: Page_180]

the first site. This could be related to the soil characteristics of the second site which is [SOURCE: Page_180]

hydromorphic and has a higher clay content and a higher organic content (Bouteyre and [SOURCE: Page_180]

Duclos, 1994). [SOURCE: Page_180]

For both experimental sites, the estimated value of the Manning-Strickler roughness [SOURCE: Page_180]

coefficient k ranged between 1.65 and 3.54 m s-1/3. These values are less than the values of k [SOURCE: Page_180]

for bare soils that can be found in the literature. For instance, (Harun-ur-Rashid, 1990) [SOURCE: Page_180]

estimated a value of k close to 33 m s-1/3 for bare soil. These values are comparable to values [SOURCE: Page_180]

of k obtained for a cropped field irrigated by submersion. (Ree, 1949) cited by (Harun-ur- [SOURCE: Page_180]

Rashid, 1990) indicated that the value of k could reach 1.7 m s-1/3. (Bader et al., 2010), [SOURCE: Page_180]

working on the same field and the same crop, calculated by inversion the value of k on 6 [SOURCE: Page_180]

177 [SOURCE: Page_180]

irrigations and found that k varied from 2.4 to 5.3 m s-1/3. (Rasoulzadeh and Sepaskhah, 2003) [SOURCE: Page_181]

assessed the variation of parameter k in furrows in different conditions and found that k [SOURCE: Page_181]

ranged between 8 and 16 m s-1/3 and could increase by up to 30% when the vegetation [SOURCE: Page_181]

increased by 47%. (Michael, 2009) suggested a value of k=2.2 m s-1/3 for the first irrigation of [SOURCE: Page_181]

the season and 3.7 m s-1/3 for the following ones; (Davis et al., 1980) suggested a value of k [SOURCE: Page_181]

ranging from 3.3 to 6.7 m s-1/3 for a field planted with alfalfa and irrigated with border [SOURCE: Page_181]

irrigation. (Robertson et al., 2004) found that k varied from 2.8 to 5 m s-1/3 for a field sown [SOURCE: Page_181]

with perennial pasture. In conclusion, our fitted values of k can be considered coherent with [SOURCE: Page_181]

values found in the literature. [SOURCE: Page_181]

For the second site, the values of k were close to 1.65 m s-1/3, which is significantly less than [SOURCE: Page_181]

the values obtained on the first site. From a hydraulic point of view, the hydraulic friction of [SOURCE: Page_181]

the flow increases as the discharge applied decreases (Rasoulzadeh and Sepaskhah, 2003); the [SOURCE: Page_181]

field surface and vegetation stage in the second experimental site were close to those in the [SOURCE: Page_181]

first one, but the applied discharges during the irrigations B$_6$ and B$_7$ were 50% lower than [SOURCE: Page_181]

those applied in A$_1$, A$_2$ and A$_3$ and 30% lower than in A$_4$ and A$_5$, which may explain the [SOURCE: Page_181]

lower value of k. [SOURCE: Page_181]

For the parameter H$_0$, it is difficult to assess the coherence of the estimation results due to the [SOURCE: Page_181]

difficulty in measuring this parameter. The measurement of the soil surface storage, also [SOURCE: Page_181]

named maximum depressional storage, is a very difficult task and this value is usually [SOURCE: Page_181]

obtained from various soil roughness indices that are easier to measure on bare soils [SOURCE: Page_181]

(Kamphorst et al., 2000). The simulated values of this parameter reported in (Kamphorst et [SOURCE: Page_181]

al., 2000; Kamphorst and Duval, 2001) ranged from 0 mm to 30 mm and from 0.26 to 48.0 [SOURCE: Page_181]

respectively. For a random soil roughness, the slope is expected to reduce the soil surface [SOURCE: Page_181]

storage (Álvarez-Mozos et al., 2011), whereas the vegetation is expected to increase this value [SOURCE: Page_181]

(Paz-Ferreiro et al., 2008). For our experimental conditions, the soil is very flat with a very [SOURCE: Page_181]

smooth microrelief, as the soil was leveled several years ago and is never tilled. Therefore a 178 [SOURCE: Page_181]

low value of H$_0$ is expected, which is the case. Despite the high uncertainty in the estimation [SOURCE: Page_182]

of the value of H$_0$, the fitted values ensured a good fitting of proxy variables for all irrigations. [SOURCE: Page_182]

3.3.Verification of the performance of the proposed approach for the parameters [SOURCE: Page_182]

estimation [SOURCE: Page_182]

Fitted values and measured values of  are compared on Fig. (3). We find a close [SOURCE: Page_182]

relationship between measured and fitted data. The measured values were calculated as the [SOURCE: Page_182]

average of three gravimetric profiles in the upstream section and three other gravimetric [SOURCE: Page_182]

profiles in the downstream section of the plot. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Figure (3) : Comparison between fitted (X-axis) and measured (Y-axis) soil water depletion [SOURCE: Page_182]

in the two experimental sites and for all irrigations used during the fitting process. The [SOURCE: Page_182]

continuous line is the 1:1 line, black diamonds are for the first site, black circles are for the [SOURCE: Page_182]

second site. [SOURCE: Page_182]

The gravimetric water content was converted into volumetric water content using the [SOURCE: Page_182]

measured bulk density and was corrected according to the stone content of the soil measured [SOURCE: Page_182]

on two pits performed on an adjacent plot. Therefore the measured volumetric water content [SOURCE: Page_182]

may not be representative of the average water content of the whole plot. This could explain [SOURCE: Page_182]

the small discrepancy between measured and fitted values of water content. Nevertheless, the [SOURCE: Page_182]

agreement is very good. 179 [SOURCE: Page_182]

The comparison between the observed runoff hydrographs, which were monitored in situ in [SOURCE: Page_183]

the upstream section and the downstream sections of the plot, and the simulated runoff [SOURCE: Page_183]

agreement in all simulated cases. The Nash criterion was always higher than 0.85 in the [SOURCE: Page_183]

the plot, the maximum RMSE value was 10.12 mm for A$_2$ whereas the lowest value was 3.6 [SOURCE: Page_183]

mm in A$_1$. In the downstream section of the plot, RMSE values were close to 3 mm for all [SOURCE: Page_183]

irrigations. [SOURCE: Page_183]

3.4.Validation of the new parameters’ estimation approach [SOURCE: Page_185]

In order to validate the approach for the estimation of soil parameters, we used experimental [SOURCE: Page_185]

data obtained by (Mailhol and Merot, 2008) and irrigation A$_4$ from our dataset which was not [SOURCE: Page_185]

used for the fitting procedure. The validation was performed on the comparison between [SOURCE: Page_185]

simulated and measured advance curves of the runoff front at the plot surface for irrigation A$_4$ [SOURCE: Page_185]

and experimental data from (Mailhol and Merot, 2008), and it was also performed on the [SOURCE: Page_185]

comparison between simulated and measured hydrographs for irrigation A$_4$. The first [SOURCE: Page_185]

irrigation from (Mailhol and Merot, 2008) occurred after the 1st mowing with a LAI about 1.3 [SOURCE: Page_185]

m^2 m-2. This irrigation is labelled irrigation A$_6$. The development of the vegetation at the time [SOURCE: Page_185]

of irrigation A$_6$ was similar to the development of the vegetation during irrigation A$_3$ in our [SOURCE: Page_185]

dataset. Therefore, we used the parameters fitted on irrigation A$_3$ to simulate irrigation A$_6$. [SOURCE: Page_185]

The second irrigation from (Mailhol and Merot, 2008), labelled irrigation A$_7$, occurred before [SOURCE: Page_185]

the 2nd mowing: the development of the vegetation was maximum with a LAI about 7.7 m 2 m- 2 . As these experimental conditions are similar to those of irrigation A$_2$, we used the 182 [SOURCE: Page_185]

parameters fitted on irrigation A$_2$ to simulate irrigation A$_7$. We used parameters fitted on [SOURCE: Page_186]

irrigation A$_5$ to simulate irrigation A$_4$, as the experimental conditions observed during these [SOURCE: Page_186]

two successive irrigations were very similar. All data are presented in Fig. (6) and Table (8). [SOURCE: Page_186]

## 2. CONCLUSIONS

The main objective of this study was to develop a new method to estimate soil hydrodynamic [SOURCE: Page_187]

parameters and soil surface hydraulic parameters at the field scale by combining inverse [SOURCE: Page_187]

modeling and flooding irrigation experiments. The experimental procedure was based on the [SOURCE: Page_187]

measurement of the hydrograph during flooding irrigation in two cross sections of the [SOURCE: Page_187]

irrigated field, one section located in the upstream part of the plot and the other section [SOURCE: Page_187]

184 [SOURCE: Page_187]

located in the downstream part of the plot. Some key variables, called “proxy variables” in [SOURCE: Page_188]

this paper, were calculated from the hydrographs and were used in the inverse modelling [SOURCE: Page_188]

approach. These variables comprised enough information to estimate the soil parameters with [SOURCE: Page_188]

the inverse modelling approach. [SOURCE: Page_188]

The performance of the proposed method was evaluated in three steps. The first step was [SOURCE: Page_188]

dedicated to checking the coherence of the fitted parameters for six different irrigation events. [SOURCE: Page_188]

The second step was dedicated to verification of the inverse modelling approach. We used [SOURCE: Page_188]

additional data not incorporated in the fitting process but obtained during the same irrigations [SOURCE: Page_188]

as the ones used for the inverse modelling approach. Error criteria such as the Nash [SOURCE: Page_188]

coefficient and the RMSE were calculated. Finally the validation step was carried out using [SOURCE: Page_188]

observed data obtained during another irrigation experiment and two other irrigations [SOURCE: Page_188]

monitored by (Mailhol and Merot, 2008). The same error criteria were calculated and were [SOURCE: Page_188]

similar to the ones calculated during the verification step. To our knowledge and as far as [SOURCE: Page_188]

surface irrigation is concerned, this is the first time that such a fitting process has been [SOURCE: Page_188]

validated in three successive steps and with data obtained from other authors. [SOURCE: Page_188]

The results revealed that the proposed method is robust and effective in estimating the [SOURCE: Page_188]

saturated hydraulic conductivity Ks and the Manning-Strickler roughness coefficient k with a [SOURCE: Page_188]

high accuracy. The soil surface depressional storage coefficient H$_0$ was estimated with a large [SOURCE: Page_188]

uncertainty, but this parameter has only a slight effect on the irrigation dynamic and the water [SOURCE: Page_188]

balance at the field scale during one irrigation. The soil water depletion  was estimated [SOURCE: Page_188]

with a moderate uncertainty. [SOURCE: Page_188]

The proposed methodology proved to perform well in estimating the main soil parameters [SOURCE: Page_188]

governing flooding during the irrigation process. These parameters can then be used in the [SOURCE: Page_188]

CALHY runoff-infiltration model (Bader et al., 2010) first to analyze current irrigation [SOURCE: Page_188]

185 [SOURCE: Page_188]

practices and then to propose ways of irrigation optimization in order to increase the water [SOURCE: Page_189]

efficiency of surface irrigation systems and to help save water. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Chapitre 3 : Utilisation D’un Modèle A Base Physique Pour L’Optimisation Multicritères De L’irrigation Des Prairies Par Submersion [SOURCE: Page_194]

La méthodologie d’estimation des paramètres de Calhy proposée et validée par les deux précédents chapitres permet d’estimer les quantités d’eau apportées et infiltrée à l’échelle d’une parcelle agricole. Cela permet d’utiliser notre modèle pour analyser ce système d’irrigation en fonction de caractéristiques telles que la dimension parcellaire et le mode d’injection de l’eau d’irrigation. Il peut aussi être utilisé pour optimiser le fonctionnement du système d’irrigation en étudiant des scénarios qui pourront être évalués selon différents critères : la consommation d’eau, la perte d’eau, l’homogénéité de la distribution de l’eau infiltrée, le temps du travail et le nombre de déplacement, le coût d’aménagement requis à faire. [SOURCE: Page_194]

Deux fonctionnalités ont été ajoutées au modèle Calhy dans la version Matlab permettant d’établir différents scénarios d’optimisation : [SOURCE: Page_194]

1. le critère d’arrêt de l’irrigation : [SOURCE: Page_194]

Ce critère a été défini d’abord en observant les irrigations lors des expérimentations d’irrigation. Nous avons observé que l’injection de l’eau d’irrigation est stoppée lorsque la lame d’eau atteint une certaine distance de l’extrémité de la parcelle (pourcentage de la longueur de la parcelle). L’eau continue à ruisseler pour irriguer la partie restante de la parcelle sous l’effet de la pente et du débit ruisselé. Trois objectifs doivent être satisfaits en appliquant ce critère;: [SOURCE: Page_194]

-     minimiser le temps de travail ; -     minimiser l’eau perdue en aval des parcelles (« colature »). -     recharger totalement la RU des sols et mieux contrôler le drainage [SOURCE: Page_194]

Tous les scénarios étudiés sont établis en appliquant ce critère. [SOURCE: Page_194]

2. l’injection de l’eau dans la parcelle latéralement : [SOURCE: Page_194]

Tout ou partie du débit entrant dans la parcelle peut être injecté latéralement sur une certaine distance. Ceci permet de prendre en compte des configurations d’irrigation qui diffèrent de la configuration « standard ». [SOURCE: Page_194]

191 [SOURCE: Page_194]

Cette modification a été effectuée en rajoutant un terme source à l’équation (19). Si un apport d’eau complémentaire, correspondant à un débit noté Ql (m^3 s-1), est effectué sur la parcelle entre les abscisses xi et xj, alors l’équation (19) devient : [SOURCE: Page_195]

+           =   ( , )−                              (43) [SOURCE: Page_195]

où ql(x,t) est le débit par unité de surface (m s-1) injecté latéralement à l’abscisse x au temps t et définit par : [SOURCE: Page_195]

( ) ( , )=                           si   ∈    , ∙       − [SOURCE: Page_195]

( , ) = 0 sinon [SOURCE: Page_195]

où Ql est le débit latéral (m^3 s-1) et l est la largeur de la parcelle. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Dans l’analyse qui suit, cinq scénarios d’optimisation ont été étudiés et évalués par rapport au mode classique de l’irrigation gravitaire. [SOURCE: Page_195]

1. Le scénario traditionnel : il est choisi comme un scénario de référence par rapport auquel on évalue les autres scénarios. L’eau est injectée en amont de la parcelle et va ensuite se propager dans la longueur vers l’aval. L’irrigation est arrêtée lorsque la lame d’eau arrive au pourcentage de la longueur prédéfinie par le critère d’arrêt. 2. Cassure de la pente : ce scénario a pour objectif d’homogénéiser la distribution instantanée de l’eau à la surface du sol, et donc d’homogénéiser l’infiltration et le drainage, en accélérant l’avancement de l’eau en amont et en la ralentissant en aval. Cela peut s’effectuer en augmentant la pente dans le premier tiers amont de la parcelle et en réduisant la pente dans les deux tiers restant. Ce scénario nécessite un nouveau nivellement de la parcelle mais ne modifie ni le temps de travail nécessaire à l’irrigation, ni les infrastructures hydrauliques d’amenée et d’ évacuation de l’eau d’irrigation (filioles, martelières, colature). 3. Augmentation du nombre de points d’injection du débit d’irrigation : la totalité du débit d’irrigation est injecté successivement en deux points de la parcelle : en amont de la parcelle, puis en un point situé aux ¾ de la longueur totale. Ce scénario nécessite d’appliquer deux fois le critère d’arrêt : une première fois pour l’irrigation des ¾ amont de [SOURCE: Page_195]

192 [SOURCE: Page_195]

la parcelle, une seconde fois pour l’irrigation du ¼ aval. Il permet donc de réduire la durée d’injection de l’eau d’irrigation. Il implique la création de nouvelles infrastructures hydrauliques. Le temps de travail nécessaire à l’irrigation augmente car l’injection d’eau est à réaliser en deux fois. 4. Augmentation du nombre de points d’injection du débit d’irrigation : ce scénario est une variante du scénario précédent, le second point d’injection étant situé à la moitié de la longueur totale de la parcelle et non aux ¾ de la longueur. 5. Fragmentation du débit d’irrigation : le débit d’irrigation total est fractionné en deux sous- débits égaux, les sous débits étant injectés simultanément en amont et à la moitié de la parcelle. Comme les scénarios 3 et 4, il implique la création de nouvelles infrastructures hydrauliques et la création d’un partiteur de débit, mais il n’augmente pas le temps de travail nécessaire à l’irrigation. 6. Changement de la pente de la parcelle permettant de passer d’une irrigation dans le sens de la longueur à une irrigation dans le sens de la largeur. La parcelle est divisée dans le sens de la longueur en N tronçons égaux, ce qui va créer N parcelles de plus petite taille. Chaque sous-parcelle est alors irriguée selon le scénario classique. Ce scénario implique un nivellement total de la parcelle, la création de nouvelles infrastructures hydrauliques, et une augmentation du temps de travail [SOURCE: Page_196]

Les scénarios proposés ont été étudiés afin de quantifier leur impact sur plusieurs critères : [SOURCE: Page_196]

1.       le temps nécessaire pour irriguer, a. 100 m de longueur de la parcelle (la vitesse d’avancement moyenne), b. toute la longueur de la parcelle, 2.       la dose d’irrigation injectée à la surface de la parcelle, 3.       la quantité d’eau drainée, [SOURCE: Page_196]

puis ils ont été classés selon différents critères d’évaluation : 1. l’homogénéité de la distribution du drainage, 2. le temps d’arrosage, 3. le coût d’aménagement requis. [SOURCE: Page_196]

Ce chapitre a été présenté comme article de conférence à la Journée Scientifique de GFHN qui a eu lieu le 23/11/2015 à Marne-La-Vallée. [SOURCE: Page_196]

## UTILISATION D’UN MODÈLE A BASE PHYSIQUE POUR L’OPTIMISATION MULTICRITÈRES DE L’IRRIGATION DES PRAIRIES PAR SUBMERSION

ALKASSEM ALOSMAN M. (1), RUY S.(1), OLIOSO A. (1), FLAMAIN F. (1), LECHARPENTIER P. (1), BUIS S. (1), BADER J.C. (2), CHARRON F. (3), BELAUD G. (3), BAILLIEUX A. (1, 4) [SOURCE: Page_197]

(1): INRA, UMR EMMAH, www.umr-emmah.fr (2): IRD, UMR G-Eau, www.g-eau.net (3) : SupAgro-M, UMR G-Eau, www.supagro.fr (4) : SYMCRAU, www.symcrau.com [SOURCE: Page_197]

Résumé: [SOURCE: Page_197]

Dans la région de la Crau, les prairies permanentes bénéficiant d’une AOP « foin de Crau » sont irriguées de façon gravitaire par submersion, à partir d’eau prélevée dans la Durance. Ce système d’irrigation participe très majoritairement (entre 66% et 80% selon les estimations) à la recharge de la nappe souterraine, ressource importante pour l’eau potable, l’agriculture et l’industrie. Face à une possible pénurie d’eau pour l’agriculture à cause des changements climatiques ou des changements d’affectation de la ressource hydrique prélevée dans la Durance, des scénarios d’optimisation pour l’irrigation gravitaire des prairies ont été réalisés à l’aide d’un modèle à base physique (modèle CALHY, [7]) et ont été évalués selon différents critères (coût, consommation d’eau) afin de réduire les quantités d’eau apportées, minimiser le temps d’irrigation et améliorer l’homogénéité de la quantité d’eau drainée à l’échelle de la parcelle. Nous avons modélisé un critère d’arrêt de l’irrigation en fonction de la longueur de la parcelle. Ce critère a été appliqué à l’ensemble des scénarios testés. Un scénario optimal est identifié : l’injection successive du débit d’irrigation en deux points de la parcelle (en amont et à mi longueur) peut réduire de 50% les quantités d’eau apportées par rapport au mode traditionnel pour un faible coût d’aménagement. [SOURCE: Page_197]

Mots clés : irrigation gravitaire, prairie, Crau, modèle d’irrigation, scénario d’optimisation, drainage, infiltration, conductivité hydraulique à saturation, coefficient de Manning. [SOURCE: Page_197]

Abstract: [SOURCE: Page_197]

In the Crau region (southern of France), the permanent grassland benefitting of a Certified Origin Product (COP) « foin de Crau » are irrigated by flooding irrigation, with water taken 194 [SOURCE: Page_197]

from the Durance river. This system of irrigation participates strongly to the recharge of the Crau aquifer (between 66% and 80% of the recharge depending on the estimations) which is an important resource for drinking water, agricultural water and water for industry. Facing a lower availability of water for agriculture because of climate change or allocation changes of the Durance water resource, optimization scenarios for the irrigation system were conducted using a physically based model (Calhy model, [7]). They were evaluated according to different criteria (cost, water consumption) in order to decrease the irrigation time and the amount of drained water and to improve its homogeneity at the plot scale. First we modeled a criterion to stop the irrigation depending on the plot length. This criterion was applied to all tested scenarios. An optimal scenario was identified: supplying water irrigation at two successive points of the plot (upstream and middle of the plot) could save around 50% of water compared to the traditional irrigation with a low cost of management. [SOURCE: Page_198]

Keys words: flooding irrigation, Crau, irrigation model, optimization scenario, drainage, infiltration, saturated hydraulic conductivity, Manning coefficient. [SOURCE: Page_198]
