## 1. INTRODUCTION

L’irrigation gravitaire est un système traditionnel largement répandu au niveau mondial, reconnu comme étant fortement consommateur d’eau. Des volumes conséquents sont apportés à la parcelle, qui génèrent des flux d’eau importants aux limites de la parcelle (drainage, ruissellement en aval de la parcelle). Ces flux sont considérés comme des pertes à éviter bien que ces flux d’eau non utilisés sur la parcelle puissent générer des externalités positives (alimentation de zones humides, recharge de la nappe souterraine). Dans la région de la Crau située dans le sud de la France, près de 14 000 ha de prairies permanentes sont arrosées par submersion. Le foin produit bénéficie d’une AOP indissociable du mode d’irrigation utilisé. L’eau utilisée est prélevée dans la Durance. À partir de la ville de Salon de Provence, plusieurs réseaux de canaux distribuent l’eau directement aux exploitations agricoles. Ils sont gérés par des Associations Syndicales Autorisées (ASA) qui contrôlent le respect des droits d’eau, des tours d’eau, les quantités utilisées par rapport la taille de l’exploitation [1] [2] [3] [4]. [SOURCE: Page_199]

Figure 1 : la plaine de la Crau, la limite de la nappe souterraine, les prairies irrigués et le réseau des canaux principaux de distribution de l’eau d’irrigation aux exploitations agricole. [SOURCE: Page_199]

Dans ce système agricole, les quantités d’eau apportées en tête de parcelle sont très importantes, de l’ordre de 15000 à 20000 m^3 ha-1 an-1 [6]. L’irrigation gravitaire participe majoritairement à la recharge de la nappe de la Crau, entre 66% et 80% selon les estimations [8], qui est considérée comme une importante ressource pour l’alimentation en eau potable 196 [SOURCE: Page_199]

(250 000 habitants), pour alimenter des activités industrielles et enfin comme eau d’irrigation en particulier pour les autres productions agricoles [4] [5]. [SOURCE: Page_200]

Ce système d’irrigation traditionnel peut être remis en cause dans le futur du fait d’une moindre disponibilité en eau dédiée à l’irrigation. Les changements climatiques futurs vont modifier le régime hydraulique de la Durance [1] [9], ce qui peut modifier les ressources disponibles. [SOURCE: Page_200]

Des changements d’affectation de la ressource hydrique de la Durance en direction d’autres systèmes d’activités (tourisme, production d’énergie) ou en vue d’augmenter la restitution au milieu naturel [10] peuvent être décidés. Des contraintes politiques et réglementaires peuvent également s’exercer sur les agriculteurs pour les inciter à diminuer les quantités d’eau utilisées [10]. Par ailleurs, une diminution trop importante des quantités d’eau drainées sous les prairies pourrait conduire à diminuer l’exploitabilité de l’aquifère. De plus, l’AOC foin de Crau rend actuellement obligatoire l’irrigation des parcelles par submersion [11]. [SOURCE: Page_200]

Au niveau des exploitations, la tendance actuelle vise le regroupement et l’allongement des parcelles afin de limiter le temps de travail de l’arrosant, le coût de la main d’œuvre étant considéré comme un facteur important pour la durabilité économique des exploitations. Ces évolutions de la structure des exploitations peuvent impacter le bilan hydrique au niveau de la parcelle ou de la région, sans que cela soit clairement documenté. [SOURCE: Page_200]

L’objectif de ce travail est de tester différents scénarios de changement des pratiques d’irrigation à l’échelle de la parcelle et de quantifier, à l’aide d’une représentation mécaniste de l’irrigation gravitaire, leurs impacts sur plusieurs critères portant sur (i) le bilan hydrique et le temps d’irrigation à l’échelle de la parcelle, (ii) l’homogénéité du drainage, (iii) le nombre d’interventions de l’arrosant considéré comme un indicateur du temps de travail, (iv) une estimation qualitative du coût d’aménagement des infrastructures à réaliser. [SOURCE: Page_200]

## 2. MATERIELS ET METHODES

Le modèle d’irrigation [SOURCE: Page_200]

Nous avons utilisé un modèle d’infiltration ruissellement qui simule l’irrigation d’une parcelle par submersion. Il s’agit du modèle CALHY [7]. Ce modèle, basé sur l’approximation d’onde cinématique des équations de Saint Venant, utilise l’équation de Manning-Strickler pour décrire le ruissellement et l’équation de Green et Ampt pour décrire l’infiltration. Les [SOURCE: Page_200]

197 [SOURCE: Page_200]

paramètres utilisés et les variables calculées sont décrits dans le tableau 1. Ce modèle a été testé et validé sur plusieurs parcelles expérimentales dans différentes conditions [12]. La figure 2 montre la capacité du modèle à reconstituer la dynamique de la lame d’eau en entrée et sortie de parcelle, après ajustement des paramètres. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Nous avons utilisé des paramètres hydrauliques et pédologiques moyens issus de nos expérimentations pour effectuer nos simulations (les valeurs sont données dans le tableau 2). [SOURCE: Page_201]

(a)                                              (b) [SOURCE: Page_201]

Figure 2 : Reconstitution de la dynamique de la lame d’eau en entrée et en sortie de parcelle avec Calhy, et estimation des paramètres pédologiques et hydrauliques pour 2 irrigations sur 2 différentes parcelles. a) la 2ème irrigation au « DDM », 2) 1ère irrigation au « Prise 7 ». [SOURCE: Page_201]

Détermination d’un critère d’arrêt de l’irrigation [SOURCE: Page_201]

Dans un premier temps, nous avons modélisé l’arrêt de l’irrigation en fonction de la longueur de la parcelle afin de définir une durée optimale d’irrigation afin (i) de remplir toute la réserve utile du sol sur toute la parcelle, (ii) de minimiser les quantités d’eau perdue par ruissellement en aval de la parcelle. Cette étude a été réalisée pour 3 valeurs différentes de la conductivité à saturation du sol Ksat qui correspondent aux gammes de valeurs trouvées sur le terrain. La durée optimale d’irrigation a alors été appliquée à tous les scénarios testés. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Les scénarios testés [SOURCE: Page_201]

Nous avons testé 6 scénarios que nous avons appliqués à des parcelles virtuelles de longueur croissante, le 1er scénario étant le scénario de référence correspondant aux pratiques actuelles. Le Tableau 3 décrit les différents scénarios utilisés. 198 [SOURCE: Page_201]

## 3. RESULTATS

3.1 Détermination du Critère d’arrêt de l’irrigation [SOURCE: Page_203]

Normalement l’arrêt de l’irrigation a lieu avant que la lame d’eau arrive au bout de la parcelle. En effet, l’inertie de la lame d’eau présente à la surface de la parcelle permet d’irriguer le bas de la parcelle. La durée optimale d’irrigation est très corrélée à la longueur de la parcelle. Elle est modulée par la valeur de Ksat , plus la conductivité du sol est faible plus le critère intervient rapidement (figure 3a), et par la valeur du déficit initial d’humidité du sol , le critère augmentant lorsque la valeur de  augmente (figure 3b). [SOURCE: Page_203]

(a) [SOURCE: Page_203]

(b) [SOURCE: Page_203]

Figure 3 : modélisation du critère d’arrêt en fonction de la longueur de la parcelle pour trois valeurs différentes de Ksat (3a) et trois valeurs différentes de  (3b). 3.2 Tests des scénarios [SOURCE: Page_203]

L’impact des scénarios a été quantifié selon plusieurs critères. [SOURCE: Page_203]

200 [SOURCE: Page_203]

1.     Le temps nécessaire pour irriguer toute la parcelle Pour tous les scénarios testés cet indice augmente de façon très fortement non linéaire avec la longueur de la parcelle (figures 4a et 4b). En effet, la lame d’eau présente à la surface du sol durant l’irrigation à un instant donné diminue d’amont en aval du fait de l’infiltration dans le sol. Ainsi, la vitesse d’avancée de la lame d’eau qui dépend de la hauteur de celle-ci (équation de Manning-Strickler) va diminuer d’amont en aval : le temps nécessaire pour irriguer une longueur unitaire de parcelle est faible en amont de la parcelle et élevé en aval. Cette évolution sera d’autant plus marquée que la longueur de la parcelle est importante, ce qui explique l’allure des courbes présentées. Les scénarios 2 (cassure de pente) et 5 (alimentation en eau de la parcelle en deux points simultanément avec partage du débit) sont très proches du scénario de référence : ils ne permettent pas un gain de la durée de l’irrigation. Inversement, les scénarios 3 et 4 d’une part (alimentation en deux points consécutivement) et le scénario 6 d’autre part (arrosage latéral de la parcelle) montrent un gain réel dans la durée d’irrigation, pouvant aller jusqu’à une diminution de plus de 50% par rapport au scénario de référence. [SOURCE: Page_204]

1.2.1 Lame d’eau injectée figure (voir figure 5) [SOURCE: Page_204]

La valeur de la lame d’eau injectée se calcule comme le produit de la durée d’irrigation par le débit injecté rapporté à la surface de la parcelle. Les quantités injectées augmentent fortement avec la longueur de la parcelle quel que soit le scénario étudié. Pour le scénario de référence, la lame d’eau passe d’environ 120 mm pour irriguer une parcelle de 225 m de long, à 260 mm pour une longueur de 445 m et à plus de 500 mm pour une parcelle de 800m. Comme pour le critère précédent, on peut distinguer deux groupes de scénarios : les scénarios 2 et 5 d’une part qui sont équivalents à la référence (scen. 1), les scénarios 3, 4 et 6 d’autre part qui se traduisent par une forte diminution de la dose injectée. Le scénario 4 est optimal sur ce critère, comme pour le critère précédent. [SOURCE: Page_204]

Figure 5 : (5a) Lame d’eau injecté en fonction de la longueur de la parcelle selon les différents scénarios ; (5b) la lame d’eau injecté et perdu en forme de drainage. [SOURCE: Page_206]

1.2.2 Quantité d’eau perdue sous forme de drainage [SOURCE: Page_206]

La quantité d’eau perdue sous forme de drainage est considérée globalement à l’échelle de la parcelle (voir figure 5b). Nous pouvons également caractériser son homogénéité intra- parcellaire (voir figure 6). A l’échelle globale de la parcelle, le classement des scénarios est similaire aux classements obtenus sur les deux critères précédents. Si l’on examine l’homogénéité intra parcellaire pour les scénarios 3, 4 et 5, on observe que le drainage le plus homogène correspond au scénario 4, qui est également le scénario optimal sur les autres critères. Inversement, le scénario 5 conduit à la plus forte hétérogénéité. [SOURCE: Page_206]

203 [SOURCE: Page_206]

Figure 6 : illustration de l’homogénéité de la distribution de drainage sur toute la parcelle pour les scénarios 1, 3, 4 et 5. [SOURCE: Page_207]

3.3 L’évaluation des scénarios testés et la synthèse de travail [SOURCE: Page_207]

Dans le tableau 3, nous avons porté le classement des scénarios selon les différents critères évalués. Le scénario 4 (où l’eau d’irrigation est injectée consécutivement en amont et au milieu de la parcelle) est le scénario optimal sur les critères purement hydriques, il correspond à une légère augmentation du temps de travail et à des coûts d’évolution d’infrastructures qui sont estimés comme peu élevés, contrairement au scénario 6 qui nécessite un nivellement complet de la parcelle. Le scénario 1 (représentatif de l’état actuel) est fortement consommateur d’eau. Le scénario 2 qui suppose un nivellement complet de la parcelle afin de mettre en place une rupture de pente, n’impacte qu’à la marge les critères hydriques par rapport au scénario 1. [SOURCE: Page_207]

## 2. CONCLUSIONS

À l’aide d’un modèle à base physique qui simule la dynamique d’irrigation par submersion, nous avons défini et modélisé un critère d’arrêt qui optimise l’irrigation dans les pratiques [SOURCE: Page_207]

204 [SOURCE: Page_207]

actuelles ou futures. Nous avons évalué l’impact de 6 scénarios d’irrigation gravitaire, ce système d’irrigation étant requis par l’AOP « foin de Crau ». La division de la parcelle en 2 parties et l’injection du débit consécutivement va réduire les quantités d’eau injectées jusqu’à 50% par rapport au scénario traditionnel. L’évaluation de l’impact de ces scénarios sur la ressource en eau souterraine à l’échelle de la Crau doit cependant être réalisée. [SOURCE: Page_208]

206 [SOURCE: Page_209]

Chapitre 4 : Caractérisation empirique de l’irrigation Estimating irrigation dose over grassland borders using regression models with field dimensions and inflow rate [SOURCE: Page_210]

Les apports d’eau dus à l’utilisation de l’irrigation gravitaire dans la région de la Crau afin d’arroser les prairies sont mal connus, ce qui conduit à une mauvaise connaissance du cycle de l’eau dans cette région. Il n’existe pas de système de mesure de ces apports à la parcelle. Les quantités d’eau transférées vers la nappe sont également mal connues : les quantités d’eau d’irrigation drainées vers la nappe de la Crau sont estimées entre 60% à 80% de la dose appliquée, ce qui participe de 65% à 85% à la recharge hydrique de cette nappe. [SOURCE: Page_210]

La connaissance de la dose réelle appliquée dans ce système d’irrigation est le facteur déterminant pour modéliser l’irrigation et quantifier les comportements du bilan hydrique de la nappe de la Crau. De plus elle pourra contribuer à évaluer les effets des scénarios d’optimisation proposés visant à améliorer l’efficience de ce système d’irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Le travail présenté dans ce chapitre a pour objectif d’améliorer l’estimation des doses apportées par irrigation en cherchant des modèles empiriques de dose et durée d’irrigation, applicables au niveau de la parcelle et qui pourraient être régionalisés sur le territoire de la Crau. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Ces modèles sont ajustés en exploitant les relations entre la dose et la durée d’irrigation d’une part et d’autre part des paramètres caractérisant les conditions de l’irrigation à la parcelle tels que la géométrie parcellaire et le débit d’injection. Ces relations sont établies et testées en couplant les mesures expérimentales obtenues sur le terrain avec des enquêtes menées chez les producteurs de foin où l’irrigation gravitaire est appliquée. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Les résultats montrent que la surface et la longueur de la parcelle sont les principaux facteurs expliquant la durée et la dose de l’irrigation. La détermination de ces variables pourra être améliorée en intégrant ces facteurs avec d’autres facteurs comme la largeur de la parcelle et le débit injecté dans des régressions linéaires multiples. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Les modèles incluant le débit pour calculer la dose injectée, présentent une bonne performance par rapport à ceux qui ne le prennent pas en compte. [SOURCE: Page_210]

207 [SOURCE: Page_210]

Estimating irrigation dose over grassland borders using regression models with field dimensions and inflow rate [SOURCE: Page_211]

Alkassem Alosman Mohamed, Ruy Stéphane, Flamain Fabrice, Lecharpentier Patrice, Olioso Albert [SOURCE: Page_211]

1-     Introduction: [SOURCE: Page_211]

Surface irrigation is a traditional irrigation system widely used worldwide. This system is [SOURCE: Page_211]

recognized as being highly water consumer: high volumes of water are injected to surface [SOURCE: Page_211]

plot, which generate significant loss of water by drainage and run-off. Although these unused [SOURCE: Page_211]

water flows can generate positive externalities (feeding wetlands, groundwater recharge), a [SOURCE: Page_211]

decrease of water volume used is sought in a context of limited water resource. [SOURCE: Page_211]

In the Crau region in southern France, the Durance River supplies the water used to irrigate [SOURCE: Page_211]

the permanent grasslands which produce ‘Crau hay’, a high quality hay labeled as Certified [SOURCE: Page_211]

Origin Product (COP). The area is characterized by shallow stony soils having a low water [SOURCE: Page_211]

holding capacity, so that the grasslands often experience mild water stress between two [SOURCE: Page_211]

irrigation events (Merot et al., 2008b). Annual average of hay yield ranges from 8 to 10 tons [SOURCE: Page_211]

by hectare. Information on the Crau area and irrigated hay production can be found in [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Beltrando, 2015b; Mailhol and Mérot, 2008; Mérot et al., 2011; Mérot et al., 2008; Olioso et [SOURCE: Page_211]

al., 2013b). In this area, border irrigation started during the XVIth century. It consists today in [SOURCE: Page_211]

the submersion of the grassland fields with high quantities of water from March to October [SOURCE: Page_211]

(up to 25000 m^3 ha-1 year-1). This water is distributed in 10 to 20 times according to rotational [SOURCE: Page_211]

turns defined by irrigation associations in charge of the management of the main surface [SOURCE: Page_211]

channels (ASA: in French “Associations Syndicales Autorisées” which literally translates to [SOURCE: Page_211]

Authorized Union Association). At the farm level, small surface channels provide water at [SOURCE: Page_211]

one side of the field for the time required for the water to reach the opposite side. For a typical [SOURCE: Page_211]

field, 450 m length, about 8 hours are required for ensuring that irrigation is completed. If the [SOURCE: Page_211]

water flow is left running for too long, water may run off the end of the field. Even if the [SOURCE: Page_211]

irrigation stops before in order to avoid run-off loss, a large part of the water infiltrates into [SOURCE: Page_211]

208 [SOURCE: Page_211]

the soil and is drained toward the Crau aquifer. In the Crau area, previous studies showed that [SOURCE: Page_212]

60% or more (up to 80%) of the applied water were lost through percolation toward the [SOURCE: Page_212]

aquifer (Merot et al., 2008b; Olioso et al., 2013a; SAOS et al., 2006). The contribution of [SOURCE: Page_212]

irrigated water to the aquifer recharge was estimated between 65 and 85 % (Gillon et al., [SOURCE: Page_212]

2015; Olioso et al., 2013a; Seraphin et al., 2015). High level of aquifer recharge in irrigated [SOURCE: Page_212]

area was also found in other aquifers in the lower basin of the Durance River (Clémentz, [SOURCE: Page_212]

1999; Lacroix, 1991; Nofal et al., 2015) or in other part of the World; e.g. (Qin et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_212]

The Crau aquifer has an important role as the main source of drinking water for around [SOURCE: Page_212]

300,000 inhabitants in the area and as it aliments several industrial and agricultural activities [SOURCE: Page_212]

(Baillieux et al., 2015; Guérin et al., 2009). Moreover, the irrigation system and the aquifer [SOURCE: Page_212]

contribute to the equilibrium of several ecosystems including tree hedges and wet lands [SOURCE: Page_212]

(Balland et al., 2002). Despite these positive externalities, interrogations on the efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_212]

grassland irrigation have been raised recently due to an increase in the frequency of water [SOURCE: Page_212]

restriction for irrigation use (Baillieux et al., 2015; MEROT et al., 2011; Olioso et al., 2013b; [SOURCE: Page_212]

## SOGREAH, 2010).

Actually, the availability of water for irrigation is threatened for the next future because of the [SOURCE: Page_212]

double pressure of climate change and evolution of water use by other sectors. Water shortage [SOURCE: Page_212]

periods might increase from one out of 15 years in recent years to one out of four in the 2035- [SOURCE: Page_212]

2065 period (Baillieux et al., 2015). Future change in the distribution of precipitation in the [SOURCE: Page_212]

Durance watershed, while environmental issues such as the conservation of the river stream in [SOURCE: Page_212]

summer, and water use increase related to demographic and economic development [SOURCE: Page_212]

(electricity production, domestic water use, recreational use of water …) might limit the [SOURCE: Page_212]

availability of water for irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_212]

At the farm level, the possible increases in the price of water and in the cost of manpower for [SOURCE: Page_212]

irrigation activities are also significant when evaluating the future of irrigation. In the Crau 209 [SOURCE: Page_212]

area, it is clear that the issue of labor cost is highly significant since almost all operations are [SOURCE: Page_213]

done manually and are very time consuming. Moreover, irrigation schedules often infringe [SOURCE: Page_213]

conventional regulations of work schedule. For the last twenty years, in order to reduce [SOURCE: Page_213]

manpower cost, a strong tendency to increase the length of the irrigated plots has been noticed [SOURCE: Page_213]

(but still not documented). The increase in plot length reduces labor as less water gates are [SOURCE: Page_213]

required for diverting water from channels to the border. However, this may be in [SOURCE: Page_213]

disagreement with the objective of improving irrigation efficiency since a common thinking is [SOURCE: Page_213]

that a larger plot length results in an increase in the irrigation dose by unit of area. This was [SOURCE: Page_213]

demonstrated in the Crau conditions by (Bader et al., 2010; Mailhol and Merot, 2008) using [SOURCE: Page_213]

hydraulic models simulating water flow in border irrigation processes. They showed that [SOURCE: Page_213]

doubling the border length (from ~200 m to ~400 m) may result in increasing the applied dose [SOURCE: Page_213]

by 20% or more (in particular when water discharge was low). However, these studies [SOURCE: Page_213]

focused on synthetic scenarios for a very limited number of situations and are difficult to [SOURCE: Page_213]

generalize since restructuration of borders is not consisting in a simple change in the [SOURCE: Page_213]

geometry of the border but is also associated with modifications of the field surface, levelling [SOURCE: Page_213]

and vegetation implantation. Moreover, an accurate use of irrigation model relies on the [SOURCE: Page_213]

availability of accurate value of model parameters such as Manning’s roughness coefficient [SOURCE: Page_213]

and infiltration coefficients which are not commonly available (Khanna and Malano, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_213]

Surprisingly, while irrigation of grasslands has a central role in the water cycle of the Crau [SOURCE: Page_213]

area, only little is known on the quantity of water which is really used. There is no monitoring [SOURCE: Page_213]

of the water used by farmers, the only available information being the water right for each [SOURCE: Page_213]

farm. As a consequence, the water balance of the aquifer and the quantitative contribution of [SOURCE: Page_213]

irrigation are very uncertain. For example, the amount of water drained annually toward the [SOURCE: Page_213]

aquifer was estimated in a very large range between 100 hm^3 and 240 hm^3 which represented [SOURCE: Page_213]

between 20% and 40% of the total aquifer volume (Guérin et al., 2009; Olioso et al., 2013b; [SOURCE: Page_213]

Seraphin et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_213]

210 [SOURCE: Page_213]

In these conditions, it is very difficult to evaluate the possible impacts of future evolutions of [SOURCE: Page_214]

water availability and of irrigation practices on the water balance of the Crau aquifer and to [SOURCE: Page_214]

propose adaptations of irrigation management practices to a decrease in water availability [SOURCE: Page_214]

while preserving irrigation externalities. [SOURCE: Page_214]

In this article we analyze the relation between the dose applied to borders and geometrical [SOURCE: Page_214]

characteristics of the borders and the water inflow rate. We derive regression models to [SOURCE: Page_214]

compute irrigation duration and applied dose from these characteristics. In a second step we [SOURCE: Page_214]

investigate the possibility to use the regression model for analyzing the evolution of the [SOURCE: Page_214]

amount of water applied to irrigation blocks (group of borders) in relation to the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_214]

their spatial structure. We also try to validate or invalidate the two following hypothesis and [SOURCE: Page_214]

we discuss the ability of the model to catch these behaviors : [SOURCE: Page_214]

-   there is a strong relation between the applied dose and the border length [SOURCE: Page_214]

-   increasing the size of the border increases the applied dose by surface unit [SOURCE: Page_214]

211 [SOURCE: Page_214]

2-     Material and methods 2.1-   The study area [SOURCE: Page_215]

This work was carried out in the Crau plain, southeastern France (centered approximately at [SOURCE: Page_215]

43°35’ N, 4°50’ E). It is a 642 km² flat territory delimited by the towns of Arles, Salon-de- [SOURCE: Page_215]

Provence and Fos-sur-Mer and bordered naturally at the East by the Berre Lake, at the West [SOURCE: Page_215]

by the Rhone River, at the North by the Alpilles Mountains and at the South by the [SOURCE: Page_215]

Mediterranean Sea (Figure 1). [SOURCE: Page_215]

The plain of Crau has a Mediterranean climate characterized by hot and dry summers and [SOURCE: Page_215]

mild winters. Rainfall on average is around 550 mm per year, but it is irregular and ranges [SOURCE: Page_215]

from 350 to 800 mm. A dry period can be observed from June to September, a wet season [SOURCE: Page_215]

from October to December, a relatively dry period from February to March, and a relatively [SOURCE: Page_215]

wet season from March to April, confirming the character of the Mediterranean climate. The [SOURCE: Page_215]

climate in Crau is also characterized by frequent strong and dry wind, the mistral, which [SOURCE: Page_215]

blows around 100 days per year with speed higher than 5 m s-1. [SOURCE: Page_215]

The main soil is a calcic luvisol (FAO-UNESCO, 1981) corresponding to the old alluvial bed [SOURCE: Page_215]

of the Durance. Its thickness varies from 0.2 m to 1 m, creating heterogeneity regarding water [SOURCE: Page_215]

stress for hay in summer. In irrigated areas, it is topped by a layer of silt brought by irrigation [SOURCE: Page_215]

water. Depending on the age of the irrigation system, the depth of the silt layer varies between [SOURCE: Page_215]

few centimeters (youngest fields) to 1 m (oldest fields). [SOURCE: Page_215]

The main agricultural production systems in the Crau area include sheep breeding, cereals, [SOURCE: Page_215]

orchards, vegetables (a large proportion in greenhouses), olive trees (for oil) and hay [SOURCE: Page_215]

production. Irrigated grasslands occupy around 13 500 ha, 8500 of which classified as Certified [SOURCE: Page_215]

Origin Product. This production system is quite exclusive, as only 13% of the farms produce [SOURCE: Page_215]

hay and fruits or cereals. However, sheep breeding is associated with hay production in 37% 212 [SOURCE: Page_215]

of the farms (Merot et al., 2008a). In winter, sheep are usually grazing grassland, while in [SOURCE: Page_216]

spring-summer hay is harvested three times. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Figure 1: Map of the Crau area showing the aquifer limits, the irrigated grassland areas and the main surface water channels. [SOURCE: Page_216]

2.2-   Irrigation system in the Crau area [SOURCE: Page_216]

Different irrigation systems are in use within the Crau area. These include drip and aspersion [SOURCE: Page_216]

techniques, mainly for orchards and vegetable, but the most widely used system is based on a [SOURCE: Page_216]

traditional border irrigation technique which consists in completely flooding the fields. Field [SOURCE: Page_216]

surface is bordered, from upstream to downstream following field slope, with water brought [SOURCE: Page_216]

in from small channels. Most of this water is distributed from the Durance River to the farms [SOURCE: Page_216]

thanks to a network of primary and secondary surface canals (Figure 1). The distribution of [SOURCE: Page_216]

water to the farms and then to the fields is done according to rotational turns and water [SOURCE: Page_216]

allocations that are determined by the local irrigation associations (ASA). Depending on the [SOURCE: Page_216]

ASA, distribution turns range between 7 and 12 days. The schedules were established 213 [SOURCE: Page_216]

historically on the basis of 0.0012 m^3 ha-1 s-1 considering a continuous flow over the year and [SOURCE: Page_217]

have remained almost the same for several centuries (Beltrando, 2015b; Peyre, 1927). Thus, at [SOURCE: Page_217]

the farm level, access to water (amount, duration, frequency) is predetermined and it is not [SOURCE: Page_217]

really possible to regulate the water supply according to crop requirements. In some farms, in [SOURCE: Page_217]

order to improve water availability, part of the irrigation water is withdrawn from the aquifer. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Depending on the soil depth, turn length has a significative impact on hay yield, with a loss of [SOURCE: Page_217]

0.1 t ha-1 for an increase of 2 days (MEROT et al., 2011). The infrastructure of the irrigation [SOURCE: Page_217]

system is also very rigid as it requires a large network of small channels and gates for [SOURCE: Page_217]

providing water to each field. Each border was also leveled for ensuring a correct flooding. [SOURCE: Page_217]

All these features imply strong constraints on any modifications of the irrigation system. [SOURCE: Page_217]

This study was based on three datasets: [SOURCE: Page_217]

### 3. Irrigation monitoring experiments were performed in order to measure irrigation

duration and applied discharge for some irrigation events; these experiments were [SOURCE: Page_217]

carried out on a set of hay fields from the exploitations where surveys took place. [SOURCE: Page_217]

The first series of surveys was used to develop models of irrigation duration and irrigation [SOURCE: Page_217]

dose as a function of field and irrigation characteristics. Several regression models were [SOURCE: Page_217]

derived based on an increasing number of explicative variables. Two main variables were [SOURCE: Page_217]

computed: -i) the irrigation duration (Ti) which is an important factor for the setting of the [SOURCE: Page_217]

214 [SOURCE: Page_217]

water distribution turns and for the organisation of labour in the farm, and -ii) the irrigation [SOURCE: Page_218]

dose (Di) which is the basis for computing the amount of water used over the field, the farm [SOURCE: Page_218]

and eventually the Crau area. We assumed that the farmers were able to give us accurate [SOURCE: Page_218]

information on the irrigation duration as this information is the main driver of labour planning [SOURCE: Page_218]

at the farm scale. Indeed, labour for the management of irrigation is one of the main [SOURCE: Page_218]

production costs for hay production in the area, so that it receives a lot of attention by the [SOURCE: Page_218]

farmers. [SOURCE: Page_218]

The experimental dataset was used to evaluate the derived models by comparing estimated [SOURCE: Page_218]

dose and duration to the values measured during the monitoring experiment. The second [SOURCE: Page_218]

series of surveys was used in the last step of the study, for evaluating the evolution of the [SOURCE: Page_218]

water amount used while irrigation block structure was changing in time. [SOURCE: Page_218]

2.4-   Surveys of farm structure and irrigation practices [SOURCE: Page_218]

Surveys were performed using a direct interview with hay producers in Crau region on the [SOURCE: Page_218]

following items: [SOURCE: Page_218]

      General presentation and history of their exploitation. [SOURCE: Page_218]

      Design of the fields (field dimensions, slope, soil depth…) and the technical itinerary [SOURCE: Page_218]

for irrigation and others operations. Field dimensions were checked using the French [SOURCE: Page_218]

national geographic system (geoportail: http://geoportail.fr/) [SOURCE: Page_218]

      Water management from field scale to exploitation scale. At field scale: irrigation [SOURCE: Page_218]

flow, irrigation duration and submersion mode. At the exploitation scale: allocated [SOURCE: Page_218]

water (theoretic discharge from ASA) and water distribution turn. [SOURCE: Page_218]

The theoretical irrigation doses Di (m^3) injected to the borders were computed as the product [SOURCE: Page_218]

of the stated duration Ti (s) by the farmers by the theoretical discharge Qi (m^3s-1): [SOURCE: Page_218]

=     ×          … (1a) [SOURCE: Page_218]

215 [SOURCE: Page_218]

2.4-1. Individual field data [SOURCE: Page_219]

Surveys were first performed over a set of 69 borders among 14 farms all around the Crau [SOURCE: Page_219]

area and in a second step for 3 irrigation blocks for which information on border structure was [SOURCE: Page_219]

available at different date in the last 30 years. [SOURCE: Page_219]

Figure 2 presents the diversity of borders in length, width, surface and allocated discharge in [SOURCE: Page_219]

the first series of surveys. Border lengths (L) ranged from 62-730 m but more than 60% were [SOURCE: Page_219]

between 100-250m. Border widths (W) ranged between 20 m and 125 m with an average [SOURCE: Page_219]

close to 50 m. Border surfaces (S) ranged between 0.2 and 4.8 ha with a large majority [SOURCE: Page_219]

between 0.5 and 2 ha. The discharge allocated by ASA presented two main regimes around [SOURCE: Page_219]

0.12 m^3 .s-1 and 0.35 m^3.s-1. The largest value was mainly allocated for the irrigation of long [SOURCE: Page_219]

borders which required a high discharge in order to achieve the submersion of the full surface [SOURCE: Page_219]

of the border. [SOURCE: Page_219]

2.4-2. Block data [SOURCE: Page_219]

Block data at two sites in the study area were obtained from the farmer surveys in [SOURCE: Page_219]

combination with the field limits from GIS layer and aerial photographs. In order to assess the [SOURCE: Page_219]

evolution of water use, the evolution of field structure and surface was derived using aerial [SOURCE: Page_219]

photographs at different dates obtained from the IGN (Institut Geographique National) web [SOURCE: Page_219]

site (http://geoportail.fr/). This site offers the possibility to download photographs up to the [SOURCE: Page_219]

mid XXth century. For these blocks, farmers provided an estimation of duration of irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_219]

which permitted to compute the applied dose thanks to Eq. 1 (Table 1). Figure 3 presents the [SOURCE: Page_219]

evolution of the structure of the two blocks. [SOURCE: Page_219]

2.5-   Experimental monitoring of irrigation [SOURCE: Page_223]

Thirty-four individual irrigations were monitored from March to October 2014 over a set of [SOURCE: Page_223]

seventeen plots accounting for a large variability of agricultural practices in the area. [SOURCE: Page_223]

The monitoring was carried out using a system composed of three capacitive probes CS616 [SOURCE: Page_223]

(Campbell Scientific), each probe, 30 cm long, set in a vertical position with 10 cm within the [SOURCE: Page_223]

soil, the remaining 20 cm being above the soil surface. The probes were installed upstream in [SOURCE: Page_223]

the field, at few meters of the side where water is supplied. The three probes were set in order [SOURCE: Page_223]

to cover the full width of the stream over the field. The output signals of the probes were [SOURCE: Page_223]

recorded on a data logger with a time step of 10 seconds from the beginning to the end of the [SOURCE: Page_223]

irrigation events. They were used to determine water arrival, recession times and irrigation [SOURCE: Page_223]

duration (Ti*). The discharge rate of the flow injected in the field (Qi*) was obtained by [SOURCE: Page_223]

measurement of the cross-section of the water supply canal and the measurement of the water [SOURCE: Page_223]

velocity using a flow meter (micro-moulinet OTT) installed during the irrigation monitoring. [SOURCE: Page_223]

The dose applied to the border was compute as ∗       ∗        ∗ =       ×         … (1b) [SOURCE: Page_223]

Geometrical characteristics of the borders (length, width) were obtained from the field limit [SOURCE: Page_223]

GIS layer and the slope was measured using a theodolite. [SOURCE: Page_223]

2.6-   Data analysis : calibration and evaluation of models for irrigation dose and duration [SOURCE: Page_223]

Regression models were established for the calculation of irrigation duration and dose from [SOURCE: Page_223]

farm and field characteristics recorded during the surveys. [SOURCE: Page_223]

   In a first step, the accuracy of surveys data was evaluated using the field monitoring data. [SOURCE: Page_223]

Comparisons were performed for the duration of irrigation (Ti vs. Ti*) and the applied [SOURCE: Page_223]

dose (Di vs. Di*). [SOURCE: Page_223]

   In a second step, we investigated systematically the correlation between these two [SOURCE: Page_223]

variables and possible explicative variables related to irrigation and border structure over [SOURCE: Page_223]

220 [SOURCE: Page_223]

the 69 border in the survey such as length L, width W, surface S, discharge Qi, [SOURCE: Page_224]

discharge/surface, discharge/width, discharge  surface, soil depth Z and slope I. [SOURCE: Page_224]

     In a third step, empirical relations explaining the irrigation duration Ti and the injected [SOURCE: Page_224]

dose Di were established with the most influential factors (over the 52 survey borders [SOURCE: Page_224]

where there was no experimental monitoring of irrigation) [SOURCE: Page_224]

     In a fourth step, the duration and the dose obtained from the regression equations were [SOURCE: Page_224]

evaluated against the measurements Ti* and Di*. [SOURCE: Page_224]

The adequacy of calculated variables from the observed data was quantified using different [SOURCE: Page_224]

statistical indexes: the determination coefficient R$_2$, which indicates the proportion of the [SOURCE: Page_224]

variance in the dose and the duration that is predictable from the influential factors, the root [SOURCE: Page_224]

mean square error (RMSE) which quantifies the overall accuracy of the model and the mean [SOURCE: Page_224]

bias error (MBE) which quantifies systematic errors that would accumulate when upscaling [SOURCE: Page_224]

the model: [SOURCE: Page_224]

Results and discussion [SOURCE: Page_224]

3.1       Evaluation of discharge and duration from surveys data against measurements [SOURCE: Page_224]

Figure 4(a) shows a general good agreement between the discharge measured in the field and [SOURCE: Page_224]

the theoretical discharge corresponding to the allocation of water by ASA. The determination [SOURCE: Page_224]

coefficient is high (R²=0.84) with RMSE = 0.034 m^3 s-1 and almost no bias (-0.009 m^3 s-1). [SOURCE: Page_224]

However, some measurements were significantly higher than survey values. This was related 221 [SOURCE: Page_224]

to the interruption of irrigation in neighbour farms during the period of hay harvest and the [SOURCE: Page_225]

release of unused water that would increase the applied discharge in the other farms. In some [SOURCE: Page_225]

other situations (not present in our data), the discharge can be lower than allocated because of [SOURCE: Page_225]

a reduction in the allocated water by the ASA (up to 40% at the beginning of irrigation season [SOURCE: Page_225]

or in crisis situation of water shortage during very dry summers). A good agreement was also [SOURCE: Page_225]

obtained between survey and measured duration Ti (Figure 4(b), R²=0.73, RMSE = 6.2 103 s, [SOURCE: Page_225]

bias = -1.58 103 s). Some significant deviations were visible caused by mismanagement of [SOURCE: Page_225]

irrigation (actually the water discharge was not stopped in due time because of the [SOURCE: Page_225]

unavailability of the farmer late at night). When dealing with the dose applied to the fields, [SOURCE: Page_225]

the good agreements obtained for the duration and the discharge resulted also in a good [SOURCE: Page_225]

agreement between the measured and the theoretical doses (Figure 4(c); R² = 0.89; RMSE [SOURCE: Page_225]

=1.19 103 m^3; bias= -0.29 103 m^3). A good agreement is also obtained for the dose by surface [SOURCE: Page_225]

unit (Figure 4(d); R² = 0.72; RMSE =41 mm; bias = -13 mm). [SOURCE: Page_225]

3.3 Evaluation of the regression models over individual plots [SOURCE: Page_229]

226 [SOURCE: Page_229]

We used the data acquired during the field experiment (Ti* and Di*) to evaluate models [SOURCE: Page_230]

derived in the previous section: Eq. 5 (Ti as a function of L, W and Qi) and Eq. 6 (Ti as a [SOURCE: Page_230]

function of S and Qi) for the irrigation duration; Eq. 8 and 9 (Di as a function of S and Qi) for [SOURCE: Page_230]

the irrigation dose. We also tested the calculation of Di from Eq. 1 by using the duration [SOURCE: Page_230]

computed by means of Eq. 5 and 6 combined to the discharge (Di as a function of Ti and Qi): [SOURCE: Page_230]

Eq. 10 and 11. In these cases, the water flow appeared as a quadratic term in the equation. [SOURCE: Page_230]

Eq. 4 and Eq. 7 which were not accounting for water discharge were also evaluated with the [SOURCE: Page_230]

aim of facilitating the possibility of spatial extrapolation of the results when water discharge [SOURCE: Page_230]

is unknown. [SOURCE: Page_230]

In general, model performances were good with determination coefficients over 0.7 for [SOURCE: Page_230]

models of the irrigation duration and 0.8 for the irrigation dose when they were expressed as a [SOURCE: Page_230]

function of plot geometric characteristics and water discharge (Table 3 and Figure 6). When [SOURCE: Page_230]

water discharge was not accounted (Eq. 4 and 7) performances were lower, in particular for [SOURCE: Page_230]

the irrigation dose (Figure 6 (a,b)). Almost all the tested models presented slight [SOURCE: Page_230]

underestimations of the duration and the dose, which can be related to the data with an [SOURCE: Page_230]

increased duration (mismanagement of irrigation cut-off) and to the data with an increased [SOURCE: Page_230]

discharge compared to ASA value. [SOURCE: Page_230]

The best agreement for the duration was obtained with Eq. 5 based on field length, field width [SOURCE: Page_230]

and discharge (Figure 6(c)). For this model underestimation was around one hour (-3.6 103 s). [SOURCE: Page_230]

A large part of the discrepancy was due to a triplet of data with a duration exceeding the [SOURCE: Page_230]

requested time for irrigating the plot because the water delivery was cut off long after the [SOURCE: Page_230]

water reached the end of the plot. Without these data, performances were significantly [SOURCE: Page_230]

improved and the bias was limited to 30 minutes (R$_2$=0.82; RMSE = 5.0 103 s; bias = 1.98 103 [SOURCE: Page_230]

s). Performances were not improved when using the measured discharge in Eq. 5 or Eq. 6 [SOURCE: Page_230]

rather than the theoretical discharge. [SOURCE: Page_230]

227 [SOURCE: Page_230]

The best agreement for the dose was obtained with Eq. 10 which combined the duration [SOURCE: Page_231]

computed by Eq. 5 and the discharge (Figure 6 (d)). When considering the real discharge [SOURCE: Page_231]

instead of the theoretical discharge, performances were significantly improved as RMSE [SOURCE: Page_231]

dropped from 1.5 103 m^3 to 1.1 103 m^3 (Figure 6 and 7). Again, not considering the triplet of [SOURCE: Page_231]

data with large irrigation over-dose and using the real discharge value, Eq. 10 presented a [SOURCE: Page_231]

very good agreement with measured data (R$_2$=0.97 ; RMSE = 0.9 103 m^3 ; bias = -0.41 103 [SOURCE: Page_231]

m^3). [SOURCE: Page_231]

the information delivered by farmers in the block survey (Figure 8 and Table 4). Overall [SOURCE: Page_233]

performances were better or in the same range as for the calibration of the models and their [SOURCE: Page_233]

evaluation against measurements. However, model ranking was quite different as Eq. 10 [SOURCE: Page_233]

which was evaluated as the best model in the previous section was here the model presenting [SOURCE: Page_233]

the lowest performances. Eq. 9 presented the best performances. It also appeared that the [SOURCE: Page_234]

structure of some models (Eqs. 7, 10 and 11) led to calculated doses lower than zero when the [SOURCE: Page_234]

area S of the border or its dimensions L and W were small or/and when the discharge Qi was [SOURCE: Page_234]

high. This generated unrealistic values for the first irrigation block at its first period as it was [SOURCE: Page_234]

composed by a hundred of very small borders with area between one quarter and one half of a [SOURCE: Page_234]

hectare. Not considering this case, estimation by the different models were more [SOURCE: Page_234]

homogeneous (RMSE between 1.2 103 m^3and 1.5 103 m^3, the best results being obtained by [SOURCE: Page_234]

Eq. 9 and Eq.11). [SOURCE: Page_234]

Estimation of the overall dose for each irrigation block at each period is presented in Table 4. [SOURCE: Page_234]

A large majority of the estimations were in a +/- 10% range of the ‘measured’ data and almost [SOURCE: Page_234]

all of them in a +/- 15 % range which was a satisfactory results. No estimation for the first [SOURCE: Page_234]

period of block 1 was falling in this range. For this case the best agreement between [SOURCE: Page_234]

calculated doses and doses estimated from farmer information was given by Eq.9 with an [SOURCE: Page_234]

overestimation by 25 %. However, it must be noticed that farmer information for that period [SOURCE: Page_234]

was not as accurate as for the other periods. [SOURCE: Page_234]

When considering the possibility to analyse evolution of applied doses in time, the results [SOURCE: Page_234]

were not satisfactory since the different models provided contradictory evolutions pointing [SOURCE: Page_234]

either an increase or a decrease of the applied dose over each block [SOURCE: Page_234]

233 [SOURCE: Page_236]

4 Discussion - conclusion [SOURCE: Page_237]

We described regression models for estimating two important characteristics of border [SOURCE: Page_237]

irrigation over grasslands in the Crau area: irrigation duration and applied dose. They were [SOURCE: Page_237]

developed at the border level from border dimensions (length, width, surface) and water [SOURCE: Page_237]

discharge which were found as the most influential parameters affecting duration and dose. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Irrigation duration is a central parameter for irrigation scheduling at the farm level and then [SOURCE: Page_237]

for manpower management and cost. Most of the operations, several hundred for one farm, [SOURCE: Page_237]

are realized manually and labor related to irrigation is one of the most important costs of hay [SOURCE: Page_237]

production (more than 20% of the total cost, Martin 2008). On its side, the cost of irrigation [SOURCE: Page_237]

water represents less than 10% of the total cost and is not directly related to the quantity of [SOURCE: Page_237]

water really used but to water rights. As a consequence, there is no precise monitoring of [SOURCE: Page_237]

irrigation water use on irrigated grasslands and the quantity of irrigation water used in the [SOURCE: Page_237]

Crau area is poorly known. It is then difficult to establish an accurate water balance over the [SOURCE: Page_237]

area (Guérin et al. 2009, Olioso et al. 2013b). This is more and more problematic as border [SOURCE: Page_237]

irrigation is using a very high amount of water compared to plant needs, while the pressure on [SOURCE: Page_237]

water resources is increasing in the area and a more efficient management of water use and [SOURCE: Page_237]

water allocation between the different uses is requested by governmental agencies (Baillieux [SOURCE: Page_237]

et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_237]

It is important to note that the training dataset that we used for deriving our models was [SOURCE: Page_237]

composed of information obtained from the farmers, the irrigation duration and the [SOURCE: Page_237]

“theoretical” discharge, and not from direct measurements. Actually, irrigation duration is [SOURCE: Page_237]

well known by farmers for each border since the irrigation schedule was in general [SOURCE: Page_237]

established a long time ago in agreement with the farm structure (disposition of the field one [SOURCE: Page_237]

to another and its position along the different channels) and is repeated for each water turn [SOURCE: Page_237]

with only few deviations. The theoretical discharge corresponds to the water flow delivered [SOURCE: Page_237]

from the main channel in accordance with water right for each particular farm and ASA. The 234 [SOURCE: Page_237]

measurements performed over some of the fields were globally confirming farmer [SOURCE: Page_238]

information. Few measurements indicated higher discharges, in relation to an increase of [SOURCE: Page_238]

available water thanks to lower irrigation in neighbor farms, and higher durations due to late [SOURCE: Page_238]

stopping of irrigation at night. For each border, variations in duration and dose also occurred [SOURCE: Page_238]

from one irrigation event to another because the water flow over the border was influenced by [SOURCE: Page_238]

the amount of vegetation and the level of soil moisture when the irrigation started. Thus the [SOURCE: Page_238]

duration values stated by farmers represented a typical duration for each border (the oldest [SOURCE: Page_238]

data obtained during the irrigation block survey may be less “accurate”). Most of the [SOURCE: Page_238]

calibrated models presented a good fit with the training dataset showing a good coherence of [SOURCE: Page_238]

the information stated by farmers. This was confirmed when evaluating the models against [SOURCE: Page_238]

experimental determination of duration and applied dose and against irrigation block data. [SOURCE: Page_238]

This opens the possibility to use the models for estimating the dose applied over the different [SOURCE: Page_238]

fields in the Crau area and for providing a distributed estimate of the quantity of water brings [SOURCE: Page_238]

by irrigation in the Crau hydrosystem. However, the analysis of the evolution of the amount [SOURCE: Page_238]

of water given to irrigation blocks was showing that the different models were providing [SOURCE: Page_238]

contradictory information on the evolution of the applied dose. This indicates that the models [SOURCE: Page_238]

were not accurate enough to catch the slight evolution of water amount from one period to [SOURCE: Page_238]

another. [SOURCE: Page_238]

From our analysis it may be difficult to state which model was performing the best over our [SOURCE: Page_238]

datasets. In numerical evaluation over measured data, Eq. 5 and 6 were providing the best [SOURCE: Page_238]

estimates of irrigation duration and Eq. 9, Eq. 10 and Eq. 11 were providing the best estimates [SOURCE: Page_238]

of the applied dose. All these models included the water discharge Qi as input and they [SOURCE: Page_238]

presented performances clearly higher than models not accounting for discharge. For the dose, [SOURCE: Page_238]

the models built by considering the irrigation duration performed well. However, all the [SOURCE: Page_238]

models except Eq.9 were giving unrealistic values for small borders (< 0.3 ha) particularly for [SOURCE: Page_238]

high discharge, so that they were not suitable for calculating duration and dose in many [SOURCE: Page_238]

235 [SOURCE: Page_238]

situations, in particular in the past (as shown by block 1). When considering the possible [SOURCE: Page_239]

applications of the models it may be also stated that models including the border area would [SOURCE: Page_239]

be more robust than models including the field dimensions. Indeed many fields have not a [SOURCE: Page_239]

quadrangle shape. From these considerations both Eq.9 and Eq.11 may be considered, paying [SOURCE: Page_239]

attention that Eq.11 is only valid for border with area larger than at least 0.5 hectare [SOURCE: Page_239]

(depending on discharge level). [SOURCE: Page_239]

We wanted to test hypothesis on the evolution of applied dose as a function of the size of the [SOURCE: Page_239]

border. First, the increase of the applied dose with the field length was clearly visible in all [SOURCE: Page_239]

situations (Table 2, R$_2$ = 0.57). A stronger link was obtained with the border surface (Table 2, [SOURCE: Page_239]

R$_2$ = 0.84). Concerning the dose by unit of surface, correlations with length and surface were [SOURCE: Page_239]

clearly lower. We found positive correlations with the length of the borders (R=0.20) and the [SOURCE: Page_239]

surface (R=0.34). When considering data for the highest discharges (Qi > 0.25 m^3 s-1) [SOURCE: Page_239]

correlation coefficients increased up to 0.23 and 0.46 respectively. This was in agreement [SOURCE: Page_239]

with the common thinking of the farmers and with the results of numerical studies (Mailhol [SOURCE: Page_239]

and Mérot, 2008, Bader et al., 2010, Alkassem Alosman et al., 2015). However, these [SOURCE: Page_239]

numerical studies were performed considering a constant water discharge, while in our [SOURCE: Page_239]

experimental results the water discharge globally increased with plot length and plot area (R = [SOURCE: Page_239]

0.54 and R = 0.63, respectively): a higher discharge is requested for irrigating longer plots. [SOURCE: Page_239]

Concerning our models, all the irrigation dose models were catching this behavior (with [SOURCE: Page_239]

correlation coefficients ranging between 0.52 and 0.88). When considering constant [SOURCE: Page_239]

discharge, as in border restructuration event, only models 7, 8 and 11 were presenting an [SOURCE: Page_239]

increasing areal dose when the border length or the border area was increasing. [SOURCE: Page_239]

The evolution of the dose by surface unit and the size of the border suggest that the [SOURCE: Page_239]

restructuration of irrigation blocks may affect the quantity of water used for irrigation. As [SOURCE: Page_239]

restructuration is usually designed for reducing the number of borders it is anticipated that the [SOURCE: Page_239]

quantity of water used would increase. However, restructuration is not only concerning the 236 [SOURCE: Page_239]

number and the size of the borders. At the same time, grassland area may increase since some [SOURCE: Page_240]

tree hedges and small channels may be removed. A new levelling of the soil surface can be [SOURCE: Page_240]

done for optimizing water flow and the distribution of vegetation changes also affecting the [SOURCE: Page_240]

water flow. In fine, the evolution of the irrigation dose applied to one block may be quite [SOURCE: Page_240]

different than the anticipated evolution and a decrease of the irrigation dose is possible. [SOURCE: Page_240]

As a summary, we can conclude that if it may be possible to derive regression models for [SOURCE: Page_240]

calculating irrigation duration and irrigation dose, their accuracy may be not high enough for [SOURCE: Page_240]

monitoring changes in water use related to changes in irrigation block structure. Further work [SOURCE: Page_240]

is now required for providing a fully independent assessment of the different models over a [SOURCE: Page_240]

larger set of field measurements and in a second time for the implementation of the models [SOURCE: Page_240]

over the whole Crau area. The possible use of the different models will depend on the [SOURCE: Page_240]

possibility to inform border dimensions and water discharge over the area. [SOURCE: Page_240]
